IV.3.1
.

Furthermore, we show in Appendix
K.2
that the state of the form given in Eq. (
61
), whose squeezing matrix appears diagonally in the HG mode basis, satisfies the variance-optimal measurement condition in Appendix
K.1
, and thus, time-resolved photon counting attains an FI of
2\Delta\omega^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
.
This establishes time-resolved photon counting as a variance-optimal measurement for a broad class of pure Gaussian quantum states.

In practice, this detection scheme relies on the implementation of photon counters. Photon-number-resolving (PNR) detectors directly measure the photon-count distribution of the optical field incident upon them, giving access to the full statistics of the probe state at the detection stage. The practical construction of PNR detectors has followed two main routes, both achieving photon-number resolution of up to 100 photons: multiplexing multiple SNSPD detectors so that simultaneous arrival events are spatially or temporally separated
9
, or employing transition-edge sensors (TES)
16
, which exhibit a timing jitter of order nanoseconds. By contrast, the sub-picosecond timing jitter achieved by optimised single-pixel SNSPDs—demonstrated at
2.6
ps system jitter in
34
. Therefore, the use of two-mode squeezed states of light as probes for measuring time and frequency shifts—combined with photon-number-resolving detectors with a resolution of 100 photons and low timing jitter—enables performance beyond the shot-noise scaling and is accessible with current technology.

IV.5
Literature on time-frequency parameter estimation

A substantial body of literature is dedicated to quantum-enhanced estimation of time delays and frequency shifts, exploiting non-classical states of light including two-photon and squeezed states. These protocols are of particular relevance to quantum sensing applications such as radar and lidar. In these contexts, an estimated temporal delay directly corresponds to the range of a target, while a measured frequency shift, induced by the Doppler effect, provides information about its relative velocity.

IV.5.1
Two-photon states for time and frequency shifts estimation

Two-photon states, which are non
-
Gaussian and typically exhibit very low squeezing, offer valuable insights into the spectral and temporal engineering required for quantum metrological protocols. Two
-
photon states provide a clear advantage for probing sensitive samples
47
;
64
. The measurement of time delays, classically performed using Optical Coherence Tomography– a Michelson interferometer – found its quantum counterpart in the Hong
-
Ou
-
Mandel (HOM) experiment. The HOM interferometer demonstrated the ability to measure sub
-
picosecond time intervals
32
, laying the foundation for Quantum Optical Coherence Tomography
48
. In
41
formally applied quantum metrology tools to the HOM interferometer, demonstrating ten of attosecond
-
scale resolution with entangled photon pairs. Subsequent spectral engineering of the biphoton state further enhanced precision. For instance, using a two
-
mode frequency
-
entangled state led to a factor
-
of
-
two improvement in resolution
8
for a fixed total bandwidth. Theoretical work then showed that engineered time-frequency grid states could push resolution to the sub
-
attosecond regime
19
.
The estimation of frequency shifts has been proposed in
19

using generalized HOM interferometry with spectrally engineered two
-
photon states. The framework was further generalized by
14
, who established the ultimate bounds for estimating a broader class of time
-
frequency parameters, such as shear and rotation in chronocyclic phase space.

These results connect naturally to the findings of the present manuscript. In particular, the factor-of-four improvement in time-delay resolution achieved with a two-color frequency-entangled biphoton state
8
;
19
has a direct analogue in our framework: the same enhancement arises for two-mode squeezed vacuum states with the same spectral separation. This highlights that spectral engineering yields consistent metrological gains across different photon-number statistics — whether in the low-flux two-photon regime or the bright squeezed-light regime.

IV.5.2
Multimode squeezed states for time-frequency estimation

The time or frequency accuracy enhancement achieved through spectral engineering of two-photon states, for a fixed amount of resources, is also demonstrably attainable using multimode squeezed states, as shown in this manuscript. This could be understood as two-mode squeezed state as a "Gaussification" of two-photon (two-mode) states, but such an explicit transformation will be let for further work. Let us now exhibit related works, and additional challenges to implement our idea.

The Gaussian state considered in Ref.
53
, for example, is similar to the one discussed in Sec.
IV.3.2
and is suboptimal: its variance-term prefactor is only "1" for both time and frequency estimation, which is below the maximal value of "4". Ref.
52
studied an idler-assisted state with a Gaussian-shaped squeezing function, whose performance depends strongly on the specific form of the squeezing function and is likewise suboptimal in all cases.

Strong coherent states with a small amount of squeezing in the derivative mode, that were shown to be optimal in the limit
|\alpha|\gg\sinh^{2}(r)
Ref.
50
, were also considered theoretically for time estimation
36
and experimentally for frequency estimation
6
. According to our framework, these approaches are suboptimal due to the linear scaling with signal photon number, but can be made variance optimal as shown in Sec.
IV.3.4
by allocating half of the energy to squeezing.

We should precise finally that achieving a resolution enhancement with squeezed states, as compared to classical states, requires a substantial degree of squeezing. In practice, this is challenging to realize due to multiple sources of noise, including injection losses, thermal effects, and other decoherence mechanisms. These factors, particularly photon losses over extended distances, rapidly degrade squeezing and induce thermalization, thereby limiting the feasibility of our current proposal for long-range applications such as radar and lidar.

We now provide a simple estimate of the sensing distance. In such experimental settings, the total homodyne detection efficiency—accounting for photodiode quantum efficiencies, mode imbalance, optical losses, and equivalent electronic noise—can reach values as high as approximately 90% (see for instance
70
). Using a channel transmission under the form
\eta=10^{-\alpha L/10}
where
L
is the propagation length, and
\alpha\sim 0.2
dB/km in optical fibers at telecom wavelength 1550nm, the total transmission is limited to
\sim 12
km (equivalent to
\eta>1/2
) for beating coherent states estimation for instance (see Eq. (
44
)).

V
Second application of optimal mode estimation: Beam displacement and tilt estimation

A further illustrative example is the estimation of beam displacement and tilt, as represented in Fig.
5
. Although its mathematical formulation is essentially equivalent to that of time and frequency estimation — and therefore does not warrant a repetition of the analysis presented in Sec.
IV
, we emphasize that our framework offers a new perspective on this problem when compared to previous literature.

V.1
Beam displacement estimation

Let us consider a monochromatic aperture field
\hat{E}(x,y)\,e^{-i\omega t}
of angular frequency
\omega
in the detection plane
z=0
, which can be decomposed into spatial plane-wave modes as

\displaystyle\hat{E}(x,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\iint_{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}<\omega^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{x}\,\mathrm{d}k_{y}\,\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y})\,e^{-i(k_{x}x+k_{y}y)}.

(72)

In the paraxial regime, the mode operators
\hat{E}(x,y)
and
\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y})
in the spatial and spectral domains satisfy the commutation relations
[\hat{E}(x,y),\hat{E}^{\dagger}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})]=\delta(x-x^{\prime})\delta(y-y^{\prime})
and
[\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y}),\hat{A}^{\dagger}(k_{x}^{\prime},k_{y}^{\prime})]=\delta(k_{x}-k_{x}^{\prime})\delta(k_{y}-k_{y}^{\prime})
, and the integration domain in Eq. (
72
) may be extended to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. A beam displacement is a transverse displacement
d
of the beam in the aperture plane, corresponding to
\hat{E}(x,y)\to\hat{E}(x+d,y)
, which induces the transformation
\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y})\to\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y})\,e^{-ik_{x}d}
on the spectral mode operators.

From this, one can readily deduce the unitary operator
\hat{U}_{d}=e^{-id\hat{G}_{d}}
of the beam displacement transformation, with generator

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{d}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{x}\,\mathrm{d}k_{y}\,k_{x}\,\hat{A}^{\dagger}(k_{x},k_{y})\,\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y}).

(73)

This generator is already expressed in diagonal form; in an arbitrary basis, one would require four integrals (or sums in the discrete case) instead of two.

Small tilts of the beam by an angle
\vartheta
in the
xz
plane about the origin correspond to the transformation
\hat{E}(x,y)\to\hat{E}(x,y)\,e^{-i\vartheta\frac{\omega}{c}x}
, from which one deduces the unitary operator
\hat{U}_{\vartheta}=e^{-i\vartheta\hat{G}_{\vartheta}}
of the tilt transformation, with generator

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{\vartheta}=\frac{\omega}{c}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,x\,\hat{E}^{\dagger}(x,y)\,\hat{E}(x,y),

(74)

which is likewise already expressed in the basis in which it takes diagonal form.

a)
b)
d
\vartheta
detection plane
reference axis

Figure 5:
Two mode transforms are illustrated:
a)
beam displacement, where the beam is shifted by a distance
d
in direction transverse to the detection plane, and
b)
, beam tilt, where the beam is rotated by an angle
\vartheta
around an axis perpendicular to the reference axis. This schematic is widely inspired from
11
.

V.2
Resources and optimal states for beam displacement and tilt estimation

Let us now discuss the resources for beam displacement and tilt estimation. The common resource is the signal photon number

\displaystyle N_{S}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{x}\,\mathrm{d}k_{y}\,\langle\psi|\hat{A}^{\dagger}(k_{x},k_{y})\,\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y})|\psi\rangle

(75)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\langle\psi|\hat{E}^{\dagger}(x,y)\,\hat{E}(x,y)|\psi\rangle.

(76)

We define
I^{\text{spec}}(k_{x},k_{y})=\langle\psi|\hat{A}^{\dagger}(k_{x},k_{y})\,\hat{A}(k_{x},k_{y})|\psi\rangle/N_{S}
as the normalized spectral photon-number intensity, and
I^{\text{spat}}(x,y)=\langle\psi|\hat{E}^{\dagger}(x,y)\,\hat{E}(x,y)|\psi\rangle/N_{S}
as the normalized spatial photon-number intensity. The resources for beam displacement estimation are then

\displaystyle\bar{k}_{x}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{x}\,\mathrm{d}k_{y}\,k_{x}\,I^{\text{spec}}(k_{x},k_{y}),

(77)

\displaystyle\Delta k_{x}^{2}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}k_{x}\,\mathrm{d}k_{y}\,(k_{x}-\bar{k}_{x})^{2}\,I^{\text{spec}}(k_{x},k_{y}),

(78)

corresponding to the mean and variance of the transverse wave vector in the
x
direction. The resources for beam tilt estimation are

\displaystyle\bar{x}\,\frac{\omega}{c}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,x\,I^{\text{spat}}(x,y),

(79)

\displaystyle\Delta x^{2}\,\frac{\omega^{2}}{c^{2}}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,(x-\bar{x})^{2}\,I^{\text{spat}}(x,y),

(80)

corresponding to the mean and variance of the spatial intensity distribution in the
x
direction, each multiplied by the factor
\omega/c
.

Applying the results of Sec.
III
, the optimal QFI for displacement and tilt estimation is
(8\bar{k}_{x}^{2}+4\Delta k_{x}^{2})N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
and
(\omega^{2}/c^{2})(8\bar{x}^{2}+4\Delta x^{2})N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
, respectively. Completely analogously to Sec.
IV.3.3
, an optimal state can be constructed by squeezing two regularized modes of the basis in which the generator is diagonal. For displacement estimation one may, for example, appropriately squeeze regularized versions of
\hat{A}(k_{x_{i}},0)
and
\hat{A}(k_{x_{j}},0)
, and for tilt estimation,
\hat{E}(x_{i},0)
and
\hat{E}(x_{j},0)
. The discussion of phase-sensitive and phase-insensitive measurements is entirely analogous to that of Sec.
IV.4
. The phase-insensitive measurements that achieve variance-optimal performance are spatially resolved photon counting for beam displacement estimation, and
k_{x}
-
k_{y}
-resolved photon counting for beam tilt estimation.

V.3
Comparison with related work

The recent work of Ref.
29
also considered the problem of beam displacement sensing using a quantum metrology framework. Their approach was to describe the problem in the spatial Hermite-Gaussian (HG) mode basis, compute the generator matrix in this basis, and evaluate the
QFI via a formula analogous to Eq. (
17
) for a general single-mode squeezed state. Because the problem is intrinsically infinite-dimensional, they introduce a mode cutoff and then maximize the QFI subject to a photon-number constraint, without invoking additional
resources. They find that the optimal state is related to the basis in which the truncated generator matrix appears diagonal, a result similar to our manuscript. Specifically, their optimal state is a single-mode squeezed state squeezed in the mode belonging to the generator-diagonalizing mode basis corresponding to the largest generator eigenvalue
k_{x,\text{max}}
, achieving a QFI of
8k_{x,\text{max}}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
. This is consistent with our framework, as this state corresponds to the mean-optimal state discussed in Sec.
III.4.3
. The authors have also found that the phase-sensitive homodyne measurement of the populated mode is optimal
29
.

Our approach extends this analysis by introducing two additional resources,
\bar{k}_{x}
and
\Delta k_{x}
, alongside
N_{S}
. Although we likewise truncate the mode space, a key advantage of our framework is that the optimal states we identify are
independent
of the mode
cutoff. More importantly, our analysis reveals that the state found in Ref.
29
is not the only optimal state but belongs to a broader family: any state squeezed in a single arbitrary mode makes optimal use of the mean resource
\bar{k}_{x}
, with the prefactor
of the mean term in the QFI universally equal to
8
. Furthermore, by appropriately squeezing two modes in the generator-diagonalizing basis, one can also saturate the variance resource
\Delta k_{x}
. A particularly attractive special case is the variance-optimal state with

\bar{k}_{x}=0
, for which phase-insensitive spatially-resolved photon counting achieves the optimal Fisher information
4\Delta k_{x}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
, as shown in Sec.
IV.4
. Such a phase-insensitive measurement strategy may be experimentally more accessible than the phase-sensitive homodyne detection proposed in
29
: intensity measurements on HG modes via spatial mode demultiplexing with a multi-plane light conversion system
12
;
5
;
57
already constitute a well-established practical toolbox that could be directly repurposed as a near-optimal platform for beam displacement sensing with non-classical states.

There are various related experimental works
12
;
11
;
65
;
5
on beam displacement and tilt estimation using strong coherent states with squeezing in its derivative mode. These are examples of the general framework in Ref.
50
discussed in Sec.
III.6
. These approaches are practical due to the use of coherent light and homodyne measurements, which are readily available. However, according to our framework, they are suboptimal due to their linear scaling with photon number. However, they can be made variance optimal as shown in Sec.
IV.3.4
by allocating half of the energy to squeezing.

VI
Outlook and Discussion

In this paper, we have established that, for pure Gaussian states, the QFI of any mode parameter estimation protocol is upper-bounded by a function of three resource parameters: the mean and variance of the generator intensity distribution over the natural mode basis induced by the transformation, denoted
\bar{g}
and
\Delta g
, and the mean signal photon number
N_{S}
. We have shown that an appropriately configured two-mode squeezed vacuum state is optimal, in the sense that its QFI saturates this upper bound for any fixed values of
\bar{g}
,
\Delta g
, and
N_{S}
. Crucially, we have further demonstrated that multimode homodyne detection on these two modes constitutes an optimal measurement, in the sense that it achieves the QCRB and thus extracts all the information available in the optimal probe state. This resource formulation applies across estimation tasks: we have demonstrated its optimality explicitly for time delays and frequency shifts, and for tilt and beam displacement. We have summarized the sensitivities that can be reached with different quantum states in Table
1
, that provide a complete characterization of optimal quantum strategies for mode parameter estimation.

State

QFI

\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

Coherent state
[\otimes_{n}\hat{D}_{\hat{a}_{n}}(\alpha_{n})]|0\rangle

(4\bar{g}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2})N_{S}

Single-mode squeezed state
\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{i}}|0\rangle

(8\bar{g}^{2}+0\Delta g^{2})N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})

Derivative mode state Eq. (
69
)

(2\bar{g}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2})N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})

Variance-optimal state Eq. (
40
)

(4\bar{g}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2})N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})

Optimal state in Eq. (
34
)

(8\bar{g}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2})N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})

Table 1:
Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) for mode parameter estimation with various quantum states. The definition of the various types of optimality are given in Sec.
III.2
.

As perspectives, performing mode estimation protocol, at constant level resources, for non-Gaussian states, is a natural step as investigated in
13
;
40
. The difficulty that seems to arise however, is that for such a state, the modal and particle-number statistics can be intertwined
13
, which renders more difficult the resource comparison. On the other hand, from a foundational perspective, an important question would concern the role of superselection rules in setting the ultimate limits of precision
59
. Besides, the QCR bounds may not be adapted for non-Gaussian statistics in the non-
asymptotic regime
46
, and other bounds were developed
67
. Then, the other perspectives are to determine the optimal strategies for the loss and noise regime encountered in realistic lidar and radar scenarios
24
;
25
;
62
;
42
;
33
;
74
;
73
;
54
, where thermal noise is expected to degrade mode parameter estimation below the Heisenberg limit while potentially preserving a constant-factor quantum advantage over the shot-noise scaling. Finally, our formalism can be applied across a wide range of experimental scenarios, from estimating parameter-shift mode transformations like time and frequency shifts to analyzing nonlinear phase effects that arise in dispersive propagation, diffraction, or even reflection from accelerating or time-varying surfaces.

Appendix A
Mode transformation

A.1
The mode transform and different bases

Let us summarize the different mode bases used in this manuscript and the most important relations for the calculations that follow.

Throughout the manuscript,
\{\hat{a}_{n}\}_{n}
denotes an arbitrary mode basis. The mode basis
\{\hat{c}_{n}\}_{n}
, defined by
\hat{c}_{n}=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
, is the special basis in which the squeezing matrix — and correspondingly the covariance matrix (Appendix
A.3
) — is diagonal. Equivalently, a multimode squeezed vacuum state expressed in this basis factorizes as a tensor product of single-mode squeezed vacuum states.

The action of
\hat{V}
on
\{\hat{a}_{n}\}_{n}
is given by the transformation rules:

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{ni}\,\hat{a}_{i},

(81)

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}V_{ni}^{*}\,\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger},

(82)

\displaystyle\hat{V}\,\hat{a}_{j}\hat{V}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{M}(V^{\dagger})_{jn}\,\hat{a}_{n}=\sum_{n=1}^{M}V_{nj}^{*}\,\hat{a}_{n},

(83)

\displaystyle\hat{V}\,\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{V}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{M}(V^{\dagger})_{jn}^{*}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}=\sum_{n=1}^{M}V_{nj}\,\hat{a}_{n},

(84)

where
V_{nm}
are the elements of the corresponding
M\times M
unitary matrix
V
. Analogous relations hold for
\hat{U}_{\lambda}
:
\hat{U}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{U}_{\lambda}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}U_{ni}(\lambda)\,\hat{a}_{i}
. We also define
\hat{a}_{n}(\lambda):=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{U}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
.

The generator
\hat{G}
of the unitary mode transformation
\hat{U}_{\lambda}=e^{-i\lambda\hat{G}}
can be expressed in any mode basis. In the bases
\{\hat{a}_{n}\}_{n}
and
\{\hat{c}_{n}\}_{n}
it reads

\displaystyle\hat{G}=\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}G_{nm}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\tilde{G}_{ij}\,\hat{c}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{j},

(85)

where
\tilde{G}_{ij}=(V^{\dagger}GV)_{ij}
, obtained by applying Eqs. (
81
)–(
82
) together with
\hat{c}_{n}=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
. The generator matrix
G
is Hermitian and therefore diagonalizable, so it is always possible to find a mode basis
\{\hat{b}_{i}\}_{i}
, defined by
\hat{b}_{i}=\hat{B}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{B}^{\dagger}
, in which the generator takes the diagonal form

\displaystyle\hat{G}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}g_{i}\,\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i},

(86)

where
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{M}\}
are the real eigenvalues of
G
, ordered without loss of generality as
g_{1}\leq g_{2}\leq\cdots\leq g_{M}
. The mode transformation
\hat{B}
acts on the basis
\{\hat{a}_{n}\}_{n}
as

\displaystyle\hat{B}\,\hat{a}_{n}\hat{B}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}(B^{\dagger})_{ni}\,\hat{a}_{i},

(87)

where
B_{ij}
are the elements of a
M\times M
unitary matrix
B
, and one has
G=B\,\mathrm{diag}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{M})\,B^{\dagger}
.

Basis

Notation

Property

Relation

-

-

A.2
Bringing Gaussian states into disentengled form

We stated in the main text that any pure Gaussian state can be written in the product form of Eq. (
6
), that we will now show. A singular value decomposition of the complex symmetric squeezing matrix
f
appearing in Eq. (
5
) takes the general form

\displaystyle f=V_{L}\,D\,V_{R}^{\dagger},

(88)

where
D
is a diagonal matrix with non-negative entries and
V_{L}
,
V_{R}
are unitary matrices. Since
f
is symmetric, i.e.,
f=f^{T}
, the decomposition specializes to

\displaystyle f=V\,D\,V^{T},

(89)

known as the Takagi factorization, with components
f_{nm}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}V_{nj}\,r_{j}\,V_{mj}
.

Consider the multimode squeezing operator. Substituting the Takagi factorization and regrouping terms gives

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}f_{nm}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}^{\dagger}+\mathrm{h.c.}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}V_{nj}\,r_{j}\,V_{mj}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}^{\dagger}+\mathrm{h.c.}\right)

(90)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M}r_{j}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{M}V_{nj}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\right)\!\left(\sum_{m=1}^{M}V_{mj}\,\hat{a}_{m}^{\dagger}\right)+\mathrm{h.c.}\right)

(91)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M}r_{j}\,(\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}+\mathrm{h.c.}\right),

(92)

where we defined
\hat{c}_{j}:=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
as the new mode basis, and used
\sum_{n=1}^{M}V_{nj}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{V}^{\dagger}=\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger}
.

The displacement operator can likewise be rewritten in the
\{\hat{c}_{j}\}_{j}
basis. Using
\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}V_{nj}^{*}\,\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger}
, which follows from Eq. (
82
), one obtains

\displaystyle\exp\!\left(\sum_{n=1}^{M}\beta_{n}\,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\!\left(\sum_{n=1}^{M}\beta_{n}\sum_{j=1}^{M}V_{nj}^{*}\,\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)

(93)

\displaystyle=\exp\!\left(\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}\,\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right),

(94)

with
\alpha_{j}=\sum_{n=1}^{M}(V^{\dagger})_{jn}\,\beta_{n}
.

Combining these results, the state in Eq. (
5
) becomes

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}\,\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M}r_{j}\,(\hat{c}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)|0\rangle=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{M}\hat{D}_{\hat{c}_{j}}(\alpha_{j})\,\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{j}}(r_{j})\,|0\rangle,

(95)

which, expressed back in the original mode basis
\{\hat{a}_{n}\}_{n}
, reads

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=\hat{V}\!\left[\bigotimes_{j=1}^{M}\hat{D}_{\hat{a}_{j}}(\alpha_{j})\,\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{j}}(r_{j})\right]|0\rangle.

(96)

A.3
Relating the squeezing matrix to the covariance matrix

Here, we will relate the squeezing matrix
f
to the more common covariance matrix
\Sigma
that one encounters when working with quadrature operators

\displaystyle\hat{q}_{n}=\frac{\hat{c}_{n}+\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2}},\quad\quad\quad\hat{p}_{n}=\frac{\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}-\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2}i}

(97)

instead of annihilation
\hat{c}_{n}
and creation
\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}
operators. Note that here we work with
\{\hat{c}_{n}\}_{n}
(with
\hat{c}_{n}=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
) as in this basis the covariance takes on a simple diagonal form. The quadrature operators satisfy the commutation relation
[\hat{x}_{n},\hat{p}_{m}]=\hbar i\delta_{nm}
.
Let us introduce

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{r}}=(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{p}_{1},\hat{x}_{2},\hat{p}_{2},\ldots,\hat{x}_{M},\hat{p}_{M})^{T}.

(98)

The components of the
2M\times 2M
covariance matrix are defined as

\displaystyle\Sigma_{kl}=\text{tr}[|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\{(\hat{r}_{k}-\langle\hat{r}_{k}\rangle),(\hat{r}_{l}-\langle\hat{r}_{l}\rangle)\}]

(99)

where
\{\hat{A},\hat{B}\}=AB+BA
.

Now, consider a general pure Gaussian state

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{V}\left[\otimes_{n=1}^{M}\hat{D}_{\hat{a}_{n}}(\alpha_{n})\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{n}}(r_{n})\right]|0\rangle

(100)

\displaystyle=\left[\otimes_{n=1}^{M}\hat{D}_{\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}}(\alpha_{n})\hat{S}_{\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}}(r_{n})\right]|0\rangle

(101)

\displaystyle=\left[\otimes_{n=1}^{M}\hat{D}_{\hat{c}_{n}}(\alpha_{n})\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{n}}(r_{n})\right]|0\rangle.

(102)

The associated
M\times M
squeezing matrix expressed in the
\{\hat{c}_{n}\}_{n}
mode basis is

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\text{diag}(r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{M})

(103)

The
2M\times 2M
covariance matrix expressed in the
\{\hat{c}_{n}\}_{n}
mode basis

\displaystyle\Sigma=\bigoplus_{n=1}^{M}\begin{pmatrix}e^{+2r_{n}}&0\\
0&e^{-2r_{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

(104)

Note that our squeezing operators and the mode basis
\{\hat{c}_{n}\}_{n}
are defined in such a way that the
p
quadratures are squeezed while the
q
quadratures are anti-squeezed.

Appendix B
QFI of a multi-mode Gaussian quantum state subject to a mode transform

In what follows and generally throughout all the manuscript, all sums run from
1
to
M
if not explicitly indicated otherwise.

B.1
The general case

The derivative of a mode transform is given by

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\hat{U}_{\lambda}=\partial_{\lambda}e^{-i\lambda\hat{G}}=-i\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{G}.

(105)

Let us continue examining the derivative of the state and simplify it

\displaystyle i|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{G}|\psi\rangle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{G}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{G}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(106)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\sum_{nm}G_{nm}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{V}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(107)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\sum_{ij}\sum_{nm}V_{ni}^{*}G_{nm}(\lambda)V_{mj}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(108)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(109)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{D}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(110)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}(\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}+\alpha_{i}^{*})(\hat{a}_{j}+\alpha_{j})\hat{S}|0\rangle

(111)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}(\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\hat{a}_{j}\alpha_{i}^{*}+\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})\hat{S}|0\rangle

(112)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}(\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{S}\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{S}+\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{S}\ \alpha_{i}^{*}+\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{S}\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})|0\rangle

(113)

\displaystyle=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}\Big([c_{i}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}+s_{i}\hat{a}_{i}][c_{j}\hat{a}_{j}+s_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}]

(114)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+[c_{j}\hat{a}_{j}+s_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}]\alpha_{i}^{*}+[c_{i}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}+s_{i}\hat{a}_{i}]\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j}\Big)|0\rangle,

(115)

where we have used
\hat{V}^{\dagger}a_{m}\hat{V}=\sum_{j}V_{mj}\hat{a}_{j}
in the third line, in the fourth line we denoted the Hermitian matrix
\tilde{G}_{ij}=\sum_{nm}V_{ni}^{*}G_{nm}V_{mj}=(V^{\dagger}GV)_{ij}
, in the sixth line we used
\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{D}=\hat{a}_{j}+\alpha_{j}
, and in the 9th and 10th lines we used
\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{S}=c_{j}\hat{a}_{j}+s_{j}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}
, where we used the shorthand notation
c_{j}:=\cosh(r_j)
and
s_{j}:=\sinh(r_j)
. Now we can further simplify this by using
\hat{a}_{i}|0\rangle=0
and
\hat{a}_{i}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle=\delta_{ij}|0\rangle
, and we obtain

\displaystyle|\partial_{\lambda}\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=-i\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}\Big[c_{i}s_{j}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle+(s_{j}\alpha_{i}^{*}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}+c_{i}\alpha_{j}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger})|0\rangle+(s_{i}s_{j}\delta_{ij}+\alpha_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})|0\rangle\Big]

(116)

\displaystyle=:|\partial_{\lambda}\psi\rangle_{2}+|\partial_{\lambda}\psi\rangle_{1}+|\partial_{\lambda}\psi\rangle_{0}.

(117)

The derivative state is now in a form such that all Gaussian operators are on the left side. We see
that the derivative states split up into three different components, a two-photon, one-photon, and zero-photon component. We can examine these individually due to
\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle=\,_{2}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{2}+\,_{1}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{1}+\,_{0}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{0}
.

So, let us start with the two-photon term:

\,{}_{2}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},i,j}\tilde{G}_{j^{\prime}i^{\prime}}^{*}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{i^{\prime}}s_{j^{\prime}}c_{i}s_{i}\langle 0|\hat{a}_{i^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

(118)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{i}c_{i}s_{j}+\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}^{*}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{i}s_{j}c_{i}s_{j}

(119)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}s_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}c_{i}+\sum_{ij}c_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{i},

(120)

where we used
\langle 0|\hat{a}_{i^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle=\delta_{i^{\prime},j}\delta_{j^{\prime}i}+\delta_{i^{\prime}i}\delta_{j^{\prime}j}

Now, we come to the one-photon term

\displaystyle|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{1}

\displaystyle=-i\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}\sum_{ij}\Big[\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}\alpha_{i}^{*}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}+\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{i}\alpha_{j}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\Big]|0\rangle

(121)

\displaystyle=-i\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}\sum_{ij}\Big[\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}\alpha_{i}^{*}+\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{j}\alpha_{i}\Big]\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

(122)

\displaystyle=:-i\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}\sum_{ij}F_{ij}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle,

(123)

where we defined
F_{ij}=\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}\alpha_{i}^{*}+\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{j}\alpha_{i}
.
With this, we find

\,{}_{1}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}ij}F_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}^{*}F_{ij}\langle 0|\hat{a}_{j^{\prime}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}|0\rangle

(124)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}ij}F_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}F_{ij}

(125)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}ij}\left(\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}s_{j}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}+\tilde{G}_{ji^{\prime}}^{*}c_{j}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\right)\left(\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}\alpha_{i}^{*}+\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{j}\alpha_{i}\right)

(126)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}ij}\Big[\alpha_{i}^{*}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji^{\prime}}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}+\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}c_{j}c_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}\alpha_{i}

(127)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}\alpha_{i}^{*}+\alpha_{i^{\prime}}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}s_{j}c_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}\alpha_{i}\Big].

(128)

For the zero-photon term we find

\,{}_{0}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}^{*}(s_{i^{\prime}}s_{j^{\prime}}\delta_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}+\alpha_{i^{\prime}}\alpha_{j^{\prime}}^{*})\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}(s_{i}s_{j}\delta_{ij}+\alpha_{i}^{*}\alpha_{j})

(129)

\displaystyle=|\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle|^{2}.

(130)

Now, by adding all of these terms together, we obtain the QFI. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\left(\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle-|\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle|^{2}\right)

(131)

\displaystyle=4\left(\,{}_{2}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{2}+\,_{1}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{1}+\,_{0}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{0}-|\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle|^{2}\right)

(132)

\displaystyle=4\left(\,{}_{2}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{2}+\,_{1}\langle\partial_{\lambda}\psi|\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{\lambda}\rangle_{1}\right).

(133)

Plugging in the expressions from above, we obtain Eq. (
17
) of the main text for the QFI.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=4\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}c_{i}s_{i}+4\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{i}c_{i}

(134)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{i^{\prime}ij}4\Big[\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji^{\prime}}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}\alpha_{i}^{*}+\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}c_{j}c_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}

(135)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}\alpha_{i}^{*}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}+\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}s_{j}c_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}\Big].

(136)

The QFI in this form is quite cumbersome, and it is useful to introduce some new notation. We define the matrices
C=\text{diag}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{M})
,
S=\text{diag}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{M})
, and the
M\times M
matrix
\mathcal{A}
with components
\mathcal{A}_{ij}=\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}^{*}
and
\mathcal{B}_{ij}=\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}
. Also note that
\tilde{G}=V^{\dagger}GV
. The QFI then takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\Big(\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]

(137)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}C^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{*}SC\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}]\Big)

(138)

\displaystyle=4\Big(\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{2}+C^{2})\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+2\mathrm{Re}\left[\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{*}SC\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}]\right]\Big).

(139)

This can be seen by using the following relation

\displaystyle\text{tr}[ABCDEF]=\sum_{a}(ABCDEF)_{aa}=\sum_{a,b,c,d,e,f}A_{ab}B_{bc}C_{cd}D_{de}E_{ef}F_{fa}

(140)

for some arbitrary
M\times M
matrices
A,B,C,D,E,F
.
If
B,C,E,F
are diagonal, we have

\displaystyle\text{tr}[ABCDEF]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c,d,e,f}A_{ab}B_{bc}\delta_{bc}C_{cd}\delta_{cd}D_{de}E_{ef}\delta_{ef}F_{fa}\delta_{fa}

(141)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,d,e,f}A_{ab}B_{bb}C_{bd}\delta_{bd}D_{de}E_{ef}\delta_{ef}F_{fa}\delta_{fa}

(142)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,e,f}A_{ab}B_{bb}C_{bb}D_{be}E_{ef}\delta_{ef}F_{fa}\delta_{fa}

(143)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,e}A_{ab}B_{bb}C_{bb}D_{be}E_{ee}F_{ea}\delta_{ea}

(144)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b}A_{ab}B_{bb}C_{bb}D_{ba}E_{aa}F_{aa}.

(145)

With this we confirm the validity of the first two terms in Eq. (
137
).

For the last four terms, we consider

\displaystyle\text{tr}[ABCDE]=\sum_{a}(ABCDE)_{aa}=\sum_{a,b,c,d,e}A_{ab}B_{bc}C_{cd}D_{de}E_{ea}

(146)

Now, if
B,C
are diagonal, we have

\displaystyle\text{tr}[ABCDE]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,c,d,e}A_{ab}B_{bc}\delta_{bc}C_{cd}\delta_{cd}D_{de}E_{ea}

(147)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,d,e}A_{ab}B_{bb}C_{bd}\delta_{bd}D_{de}E_{ea}

(148)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,b,e}A_{ab}B_{bb}C_{bb}D_{be}E_{ea}.

(149)

With this, we confirm the four terms in Eq. (
138
).

As we will later see, bringing the QFI into this form will be helpful for proving our main result.

B.2
QFI of a coherent state

As in the main text, the QFI of the coherent state is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle)=4\sum_{i^{\prime}ij}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}\tilde{G}_{ji}\alpha_{i}=4\bm{\alpha}^{\dagger}\tilde{G}^{2}\bm{\alpha}=4\bm{\alpha}^{\dagger}W^{\dagger}D^{2}W\bm{\alpha}=4\sum_{n}D_{nn}^{2}|(W\bm{\alpha})_{n}|^{2}.

(150)

Now,
W=B^{\dagger}V
is the unitary that diagonalizes
\tilde{G}
(and also
\tilde{G}^{2}
). Recall that
G=BDB^{\dagger}
.

\displaystyle|(W\bm{\alpha})_{n}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}W_{nj}^{*}W_{ni}\alpha_{j}^{*}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{ij}W_{nj}^{*}W_{ni}\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{V}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{V}^{\dagger}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle

(151)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}W_{nj}^{*}W_{ni}\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{V}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{V}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{V}^{\dagger}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle=\sum_{ij}W_{nj}^{*}W_{ni}\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\sum_{q}V_{qj}\hat{a}_{q}^{\dagger}\sum_{r}V_{ri}^{*}\hat{a}_{r}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle

(152)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ijqr}V_{qj}W_{nj}^{*}W_{ni}V_{ri}^{*}\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{a}_{q}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{r}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle=\sum_{qr}(\sum_{j}V_{qj}(W^{\dagger})_{jn})(\sum_{i}W_{ni}(V^{\dagger})_{ir})\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{a}_{q}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{r}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle

(153)

\displaystyle=\sum_{qr}(V(B^{\dagger}V)^{\dagger})_{qn}(B^{\dagger}VV^{\dagger})_{nr}\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{a}_{q}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{r}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle=\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|(\sum_{q}B_{qn}\hat{a}_{q}^{\dagger})(\sum_{r}B_{nr}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{r})|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle

(154)

\displaystyle=\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{n}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle.

(155)

In the first line we used
\alpha_{j}\alpha_{i}=\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{V}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{V}^{\dagger}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle
, recall that
|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle=\hat{V}[\otimes_{i}\hat{D}_{\hat{a}_{i}}(\alpha_{i})]|0\rangle
. In the second line we used Eq. (
83
) and Eq. (
84
). In the fourth line we used
W=B^{\dagger}V
.

From this follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{n}D_{nn}^{2}|(W\bm{\alpha})_{n}|^{2}

(156)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{n}g_{n}^{2}\langle\psi^{\text{coh}}|\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{n}|\psi^{\text{coh}}\rangle.

(157)

Appendix C
Resources

In the main text we have given the definition of the resources in terms of the intensity
I_{n}
. It will prove useful to express the resources in a basis independent way.

Let us first start with the signal photon number

\displaystyle N_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}}\langle\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\rangle=\sum_{n,m}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}}(B^{\dagger})_{in}^{*}(B^{\dagger})_{im}\langle\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\rangle

(158)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}}B_{ni}(B^{\dagger})_{im}\langle\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\rangle

(159)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}(P_{S})_{nm}\langle 0|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(160)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}(V^{\dagger}P_{S}V)_{nm}\langle 0|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(161)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}(\tilde{P}_{S})_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{m}\alpha_{n}^{*}\right)

(162)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{P}_{S}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})]

(163)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{P}_{S}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})\tilde{P}_{S}].

(164)

In the third line we defined the projector onto the eigenspace of
G
with non-zero eigenvalues
P_{S}=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}}B_{ni}(B^{\dagger})_{im}=\sum_{i,j}B_{ni}\chi_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}(i)\delta_{ij}(B^{\dagger})_{im}
, where
\chi_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}(i)=1
if
i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}
and
0
otherwise. That is
P_{S}G=GP_{S}=P_{S}GP_{S}=G
.
In the fourth line, we defined
\tilde{P}_{S}=V^{\dagger}P_{S}V
, which is the projector onto the eigenspace of
\tilde{G}
with non-zero eigenvalues. We have
\tilde{P}_{S}\tilde{G}=\tilde{G}\tilde{P}_{S}=\tilde{P}_{S}\tilde{G}\tilde{P}_{S}=\tilde{G}
.

For the mean resource
\bar{g}
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{g}\cdot N_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}}g_{i}\langle\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\rangle

(165)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}g_{i}\langle\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\rangle

(166)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}G_{nm}\langle\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\rangle=\sum_{n,m}G_{nm}\langle 0|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(167)

\displaystyle=\sum_{nm}(V^{\dagger}GV)_{nm}\langle 0|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(168)

\displaystyle=\sum_{nm}\tilde{G}_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)

(169)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})]

(170)

and thus

\displaystyle\bar{g}=\frac{\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})]}{N_{S}}.

(171)

We see that this quantity only depends on how the modes are distributed, not on the total number of photons.

And finally, for the variance resource parameter
\bar{g}
we have

\displaystyle\left((\Delta g)^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{S}}g_{i}^{2}\langle\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\rangle

(172)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}g_{i}^{2}\langle\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\rangle

(173)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}(G^{2})_{nm}\langle\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\rangle=\sum_{n,m}(G^{2})_{nm}\langle 0|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{V}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{V}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(174)

\displaystyle=\sum_{nm}(V^{\dagger}G^{2}V)_{nm}\langle 0|\hat{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{m}\hat{D}\hat{S}|0\rangle

(175)

\displaystyle=\sum_{nm}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)

(176)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})].

(177)

With this, we get

\displaystyle(\Delta g)^{2}=\frac{\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})]}{N_{S}}-\bar{g}^{2}.

(178)

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
III.1

We now prove Theorem
III.1
, which requires showing that
\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)\leq\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
.

We begin by establishing two lemmas that will enable a concise proof of Theorem
III.1
.

Lemma D.1
.

The QFI given in Eq. (
19
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)\leq 4\left(2\,\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+4\,\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]\right)+O(N_{S}).

(179)

The proof relies on the Hermiticity of
\tilde{G}
and, while straightforward, is somewhat lengthy; it is provided in Appendix
D.1
. The purpose of this intermediate bound is to reduce the expression to one containing only two terms, which simplifies the subsequent analysis.

The second lemma is:

Lemma D.2
.

The inequality

\displaystyle 0\leq 4\mathrm{Tr}[HQ]^{2}+4\mathrm{Tr}[H^{2}Q]\mathrm{Tr}[Q]-8\mathrm{Tr}[HQHQ]

(180)

holds for
H
Hermitian and
Q
Hermitian and positive semi-definite.

The proof of this lemma
D.2
has been delegated to the Appendix
D.2
.

We are now ready to complete the proof of Theorem
III.1
.

Proof.

We use the lemma
D.2
and set
H=\tilde{G}
and
Q=\tilde{P}_{S}(S+\mathcal{A})\tilde{P}_{S}:=S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, where we denote
S^{\prime}=\tilde{P}_{S}S\tilde{P}_{S}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\tilde{P}_{S}A\tilde{P}_{S}
. With this, we obtain

\displaystyle 0\leq 4\text{Tr}[HQ]^{2}+4\text{Tr}[H^{2}Q]\text{Tr}[Q]-8\text{Tr}[HQHQ]

(181)

\displaystyle=4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})]^{2}+4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})]\text{Tr}[S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]-8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\tilde{G}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})]

(182)

\displaystyle=\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}-8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}S^{\prime}]-2\cdot 8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]-8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]

(183)

where in the last line we used
4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})]^{2}=4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S+\mathcal{A})]^{2}=4\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}
and
4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})]\text{Tr}[S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]=4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}(S+\mathcal{A})]\text{Tr}[S+\mathcal{A}]=4(\bar{g}^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2})N_{S}^{2}
, which follows from
\tilde{P}_{S}\tilde{G}=\tilde{G}\tilde{P}_{S}=\tilde{G}
. We also used in the last line

\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\tilde{G}(S^{\prime}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime})]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}S^{\prime}]+2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]

exploiting linearity and the cyclic property of the trace. By now bringing the first two terms with the minus sign to the left side, we obtain:

\displaystyle 8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}S^{\prime}]+2\cdot 8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]\leq\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}-8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}].

(184)

We now use the lemma
D.1
. For this, we add the appropriate terms of order
L:=4\cdot 3(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}-4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]
on both sides of Eq. (
184
) and with
\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]
,
\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}S^{\prime}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S\tilde{G}S]
and
\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{\prime}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]
we obtain:

\displaystyle 8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S\tilde{G}S]+2\cdot 8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+L\leq\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}-8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}A\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+L.

(185)

From this and lemma
D.1
follows directly:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}-8\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}A\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+L

(186)

\displaystyle\leq\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+L

(187)

\displaystyle=\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+4\cdot 3(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}-4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}].

(188)

In the last line, we used
\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]\geq 0
. This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{A}
is Hermitian and positive semi-definite. Therefore,
\mathcal{A}^{1/2}
exists and is also Hermitian. Thus, we have
\text{Tr}[\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}]=\text{Tr}[(\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{1/2})^{2}]\geq 0
. This follows from the fact that
\mathcal{A}^{1/2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}^{1/2}
is Hermitian, and the trace of the square of a Hermitian matrix is always non-negative. This completes the proof.
∎

An important observation is that Eq. (
186
) implies that Eq. (
187
) can only be attained if
\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]=0
. While this might suggest that displacement always decreases performance, there may exist special non-zero displacement configurations for which
\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]=0
, so that the possibility of an optimal state with non-zero displacement cannot be excluded
1
1

1

One can show that
\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]=(\bm{\alpha}^{\dagger}\tilde{G}\bm{\alpha})^{2}
. When
\tilde{G}
has both positive and negative eigenvalues, one can find
\bm{\alpha}\neq 0
such that
\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]=(\bm{\alpha}^{\dagger}\tilde{G}\bm{\alpha})^{2}=0
.
. Indeed, in Sec.
E.2.2
we identified a variance-optimal state that allocates half of its energy budget to displacement.

In the case of zero displacement, the bound in Eq. (
188
) reduces to

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)\leq\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4(\Delta g)^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S},

(189)

which is tight even at the level of the linear term in
N_{S}
, as can be verified by evaluating the QFI of the optimal state given in Eq. (
340
). Finally, in the limit of small squeezing, a multimode squeezed vacuum state reduces to a superposition of the vacuum and a two-photon state, suggesting that the present bound may also be applicable to two-photon states — a direction worth pursuing in future work.

D.1
Proof of Lemma
D.1

Proof.

First, recall that the QFI is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=4\Big[\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}A]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}C^{2}\tilde{G}A]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}B^{*}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{*}SC\tilde{G}B]\Big].

(190)

We have six terms and, by bounding some of them, we derive an upper bound with only two terms quadratic in
N_{S}
.
Let us start with the terms that are only due to squeezing. We have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}c_{i}s_{i}=\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}c_{j}s_{j}c_{i}s_{i}

(191)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}a_{j}a_{i}

(192)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i<j}(\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}+\tilde{G}_{ji}^{2})a_{i}a_{j}

(193)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i<j}(\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}+(\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2})^{*})a_{i}a_{j}

(194)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i<j}2\text{Re}[\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}]a_{i}a_{j}

(195)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}\text{Re}[\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}]a_{i}a_{j}

(196)

where we introduced
a_{i}=c_{i}s_{i}
and used
\text{Re}[\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}]=\text{Re}[\tilde{G}_{ji}^{2}]
in the last line. Note, here
a_{i}
does not stand for an annihilation operator. The above clearly shows that
\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]
is real-valued. We continue

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]\leq|\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}\Big|+\Big|\sum_{i\neq j}\text{Re}[\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}]a_{i}a_{j}\Big|

(197)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}\big|\text{Re}[\tilde{G}_{ij}^{2}]\big|a_{i}a_{j}

(198)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}^{2}a_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{i\neq j}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}a_{i}a_{j}

(199)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}a_{i}a_{j}=\sum_{i,j}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}s_{i}c_{i}s_{j}c_{j}

(200)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}s_{i}\sqrt{s_{i}^{2}+1}s_{j}\sqrt{s_{j}^{2}+1}

(201)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{ij}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}\left[s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})\right]

(202)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ij}|\tilde{G}_{ij}|^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})

(203)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+2\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Tr}[G^{2}S^{2}],

(204)

where we used
\text{Re}[z^{2}]\leq|z|^{2}
in the third line. In the fifth line we used
c_{i}=\sqrt{s_{i}^{2}+1}
. In the sixth line we used
\text{LHS}=s_{i}\sqrt{s_{i}^{2}+1}s_{j}\sqrt{s_{j}^{2}+1}\leq s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})=\text{RHS}
. This can be seen by squaring both sides (both sides are non-negative), that is, the left hand side squared is
\text{LHS}^{2}=s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)=(s_{i}^{4}+s_{i}^{2})(s_{j}^{4}+s_{j}^{2})=s_{i}^{4}s_{j}^{4}+s_{i}^{4}s_{j}^{2}+s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{4}+s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}
. The right hand side squared is

\displaystyle\text{RHS}^{2}=

\displaystyle[s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})]^{2}=s_{i}^{4}s_{j}^{4}+2\frac{1}{2}s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})+\frac{1}{4}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})^{2}

(205)

\displaystyle=s_{i}^{4}s_{j}^{4}+s_{i}^{4}s_{j}^{2}+s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{4}+\frac{1}{4}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})^{2}.

(206)

Now, let us rewrite
\text{LHS}^{2}\leq\text{RHS}^{2}
as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\text{RHS}^{2}-\text{LHS}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2})^{2}-s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(s_{i}^{2}-s_{j}^{2})^{2},

(207)

which is obviously true as the term after the last equals sign is manifestly non-negative. And thus
\text{LHS}\leq\text{RHS}
from which Eq. (
204
) finally follows.

Now, let us come to the terms that are due to displacement. Note that
\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*}]^{*}=\text{Tr}[(\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\dagger}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{*}CS\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}]
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{*}SC\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}]

\displaystyle=2\text{Re}\left[\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*}]\right]

(208)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*}]\right|

(209)

\displaystyle=2\left|\sum_{iji^{\prime}}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}\alpha_{i}^{*}\right|

(210)

\displaystyle=2\left|\sum_{j}c_{j}s_{j}(\sum_{i^{\prime}}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}^{*})(\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}\alpha_{i}^{*})\right|

(211)

\displaystyle=2\left|\sum_{j}c_{j}s_{j}(\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}\alpha_{i}^{*})^{2}\right|

(212)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j}c_{j}s_{j}\left|\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}\alpha_{i}^{*}\right|^{2}

(213)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{j}c_{j}s_{j}\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}\alpha_{i}^{*}\sum_{i^{\prime}}\tilde{G}_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}

(214)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{iji^{\prime}}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji^{\prime}}\alpha_{i^{\prime}}\alpha_{i}^{*}

(215)

\displaystyle=2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]

(216)

\displaystyle\leq 2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}C^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]

(217)

\displaystyle=2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}\mathcal{A}].

(218)

With

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}C^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}(S^{2}+1)\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}\mathcal{A}]

(219)

and

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]

(220)

we finally obtain with all of the results above

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\Big[\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]

(221)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}C^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}\mathcal{B}^{*}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{*}SC\tilde{G}\mathcal{B}]\Big]

(222)

\displaystyle\leq 4\Big[\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+\mathrm{Tr}[G^{2}S^{2}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]

(223)

\displaystyle\quad+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}\mathcal{A}]+2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+2\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}\mathcal{A}]\Big]

(224)

\displaystyle=4\cdot\Bigg(2\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+4\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]+3\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}(S^{2}+\mathcal{A})]-\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]\Bigg)

(225)

\displaystyle=4\cdot\left(2\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}S^{2}]+4\mathrm{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}\mathcal{A}]\right)+4\cdot 3(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}-4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]

(226)

This completes the proof.
∎

D.2
Proof of Lemma
D.2

Proof.

As
Q
is Hermitian and positive semi-definite, there is a unitary
W
, such that
Q=W^{\dagger}DW
, where
D=\text{diag}(d_{1},\ldots,d_{M})
is diagonal and we have
d_{i}\geq 0
. With this, let us consider the right-hand side of the to be proved inequality which we divide by
4
and obtain

\displaystyle\text{RHS}/4

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[HQ]^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}[H^{2}Q]\mathrm{Tr}[Q]-2\mathrm{Tr}[HQHQ]

(227)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[HW^{\dagger}DW]^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}[H^{2}W^{\dagger}DW]\mathrm{Tr}[W^{\dagger}DW]-2\mathrm{Tr}[HW^{\dagger}DWHW^{\dagger}DW]

(228)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}[H^{\prime}D]^{2}+\mathrm{Tr}[H^{\prime 2}D]\mathrm{Tr}[D]-2\mathrm{Tr}[H^{\prime}DH^{\prime}D],

(229)

where we introduced
H^{\prime}=WHW^{\dagger}
, which is Hermitian. Let us now continue by rewriting the above in terms of components. For the first term, we have to calculate the square of the following

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[H^{\prime}D]=\sum_{k}(H^{\prime}D)_{kk}=\sum_{k}\sum_{i}H_{ki}^{\prime}D_{ik}\delta_{ik}=\sum_{i}H_{ii}^{\prime}d_{i}.

(230)

For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}[H^{\prime 2}D]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}(H^{\prime 2}D)_{kk}=\sum_{k}\sum_{i}(H^{\prime 2})_{ki}D_{ik}\delta_{ik}=\sum_{i}(H^{\prime 2})_{ii}d_{i}=\sum_{i}\sum_{n}H_{in}^{\prime}H_{ni}^{\prime}d_{i}

(231)

\displaystyle=\sum_{in}|H^{\prime}_{in}|^{2}d_{i}.

(232)

For the third term, we have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}[H^{\prime}DH^{\prime}D]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}(H^{\prime}DH^{\prime}D)_{jj}=\sum_{j}\sum_{i}(H^{\prime}D)_{ji}(H^{\prime}D)_{ij}

(233)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\sum_{i}\sum_{r}H_{jr}^{\prime}D_{ri}\sum_{q}H_{iq}^{\prime}D_{qj}

(234)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ji}H_{ji}^{\prime}D_{ii}H_{ij}^{\prime}D_{jj}

(235)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}|H^{\prime}_{ij}|^{2}d_{i}d_{j}

(236)

Putting these three terms together, we obtain for the right-hand side

\displaystyle\text{RHS}/4

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}H^{\prime}_{ii}d_{i}\sum_{j}H^{\prime}_{jj}d_{j}+\sum_{in}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}\sum_{j}d_{j}-2\sum_{ij}|H^{\prime}_{ij}|^{2}d_{i}d_{j}

(237)

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\left[H_{ii}^{\prime}H_{jj}^{\prime}+\sum_{n}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}-2|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}\right]d_{i}d_{j}

(238)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left[H_{ii}^{\prime 2}+\sum_{n}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}-2|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}\right]d_{i}^{2}

(239)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i\neq j}\left[H_{ii}^{\prime}H_{jj}^{\prime}+\sum_{n}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}-2|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}\right]d_{i}d_{j}.

(240)

Now, note that we have for the sum over
n
in the third line

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\sum_{n}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i}\left[|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i\end{subarray}}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}\right]d_{i}^{2}

(241)

and for the sum over
n
in the fourth line, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq j}

\displaystyle\sum_{n}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}d_{j}=\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{n}\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}

(242)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\frac{|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{ji}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jj}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i,j\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}

(243)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\left(\frac{|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{|H_{jj}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i,j\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}

(244)

Plugging these expressions into RHS yields

\displaystyle\text{RHS}/4

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\underbrace{\left[H_{ii}^{\prime 2}+|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}-2|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}\right]}_{=0}d_{i}^{2}

(245)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i\neq j}\left[H_{ii}^{\prime}H_{jj}^{\prime}+\frac{|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}+\frac{|H_{jj}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}-2|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}\right]d_{i}d_{j}

(246)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i\end{subarray}}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i,j\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}

(247)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\Bigg(\frac{H_{ii}^{\prime}}{2}+\frac{H_{jj}^{\prime}}{2}\Bigg)^{2}d_{i}d_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}-|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}d_{j}

(248)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i\end{subarray}}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i,j\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}

(249)

where we used
H_{ii}^{\prime 2}=|H_{ii}^{\prime}|^{2}
in the first line, which is true to Hermiticity. Now, let us note that

\displaystyle\sum_{i}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i\end{subarray}}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i\neq n}|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i\neq j}|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}

(250)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{i}^{2}}{2}+\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{|H_{ji}^{\prime}|^{2}d_{j}^{2}}{2}

(251)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}\frac{d_{i}^{2}+d_{j}^{2}}{2}.

(252)

With this, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\text{RHS}/4

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\Bigg(\frac{H_{ii}^{\prime}}{2}+\frac{H_{jj}^{\prime}}{2}\Bigg)^{2}d_{i}d_{j}+\sum_{i\neq j}|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}\frac{d_{i}^{2}-2d_{i}d_{j}+d_{j}^{2}}{2}

(253)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i,j\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}

(254)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq j}\underbrace{\Bigg(\frac{H_{ii}^{\prime}}{2}+\frac{H_{jj}^{\prime}}{2}\Bigg)^{2}d_{i}d_{j}}_{\geq 0}+\sum_{i\neq j}\underbrace{|H_{ij}^{\prime}|^{2}\frac{(d_{i}-d_{j})^{2}}{2}}_{\geq 0}

(255)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
n\neq i,j\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(\frac{|H_{in}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{|H_{jn}^{\prime}|^{2}}{2}\right)d_{i}d_{j}}_{\geq 0}

(256)

\displaystyle\geq 0.

(257)

Note that
H_{ii}^{\prime}
is real (due to Hermiticity of
H
), and
d_{i}\geq 0
(due to positive semi-definiteness of
Q
). Thus, the three above terms are in a manifestly non-negative form. Thus, we have
\text{RHS}\geq 0
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Optimal states

E.1
Mean-optimal state

We will now discuss the scenario in which we aim to maximize the prefactor of the term proportional to
\bar{g}^{2}
in the QFI. We refer to such a states as mean optimal.

As an Ansatz, we choose a single-mode squeezed state with zero displacement. Thus, the input state we consider takes the form

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=\hat{V}\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{n}}(r_{n})|0\rangle=\hat{S}_{\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}}(r_{n})|0\rangle.

(258)

Thus, the single mode
\hat{c}_{n}=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
is squeezed with squeezing parameter
r_{n}>0
. The other modes are in vacuum, i.e.,
r_{i}=0
for
i\neq n
.
Also note that
\hat{V}
shall be completely arbitrary.

The calculation of the QFI of such a state is straightforward.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]

(259)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{ij}s_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}c_{i}+4\sum_{ij}c_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{i}

(260)

\displaystyle=4s_{n}\tilde{G}_{nn}c_{n}s_{n}\tilde{G}_{nn}^{*}c_{n}+4\sum_{i}c_{i}\tilde{G}_{in}s_{n}s_{n}\tilde{G}_{ni}c_{i}

(261)

\displaystyle=4|\tilde{G}_{nn}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}c_{n}^{2}+4|\tilde{G}_{nn}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}c_{n}^{2}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\\
i\neq n\end{subarray}}|\tilde{G}_{in}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}c_{i}^{2}

(262)

\displaystyle=8|\tilde{G}_{nn}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}(s_{n}^{2}+1)+4\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\\
i\neq n\end{subarray}}|\tilde{G}_{in}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}\cdot 1

(263)

\displaystyle=8|\tilde{G}_{nn}|^{2}s_{n}^{4}+4\sum_{i}|\tilde{G}_{in}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}.

(264)

In the third line, we used
s_{i}=0
for
i\neq n
, in the fourth line we separated the
n
term from the sum, in the fifth line we used
c_{i}^{2}=s_{i}^{2}+1
for all
i
and
c_{i}=1
for
i\neq n
.

Now, we want to relate this result for the QFI to the resources
N_{S}
,
\bar{g}
, and
\Delta g
.
We have

\displaystyle N_{S}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{P}_{S}S^{2}]=\sum_{i}(\tilde{P}_{S}S^{2})_{ii}=\sum_{i,j}(\tilde{P}_{S})_{ij}(S^{2})_{ji}=\sum_{i,j}(\tilde{P}_{S})_{ij}s_{i}^{2}\delta_{ji}=\sum_{i}(\tilde{P}_{S})_{ii}s_{i}^{2}

(265)

\displaystyle=(\tilde{P}_{S})_{nn}s_{n}^{2}.

(266)

Recall that
\tilde{P}_{S}
is the projector onto the subspace spanned by the eigenvectors of
\tilde{G}
associated to non-zero eigenvalues. In the (common) case where
\tilde{G}
has only non-zero eigenvalues,
\tilde{P}_{S}
is simply the identity.

For the mean resource parameter we have

\displaystyle\bar{g}N_{S}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}]=\sum_{ij}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{i}^{2}\delta_{ij}=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ii}s_{i}^{2}=\tilde{G}_{nn}s_{n}^{2}

(267)

We also have

\displaystyle\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]=\sum_{nm}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nm}s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}=\sum_{n}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nn}s_{n}^{2}

(268)

\displaystyle=\sum_{in}\tilde{G}_{ni}\tilde{G}_{in}s_{n}^{2}=\sum_{in}|\tilde{G}_{in}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}

(269)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}|\tilde{G}_{in}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}.

(270)

By examining the QFI in Eq. (
264
) and the expressions for
\bar{g}N_{S}
and
\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}
, we recognize that the QFI can be rewritten in terms of the resources parameters:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+0\cdot\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}+4(\Delta g)^{2}N_{S}

(271)

\displaystyle=8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+0\cdot\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S}).

(272)

Thus, an arbitrary single mode squeezed state attains the best prefactor of
8
in the
\bar{g}^{2}
term, but the worst prefactor of
0
for the
(\Delta g)^{2}
term. It is an optimal state if
\Delta g=0
or almost optimal in the case of
\bar{g}^{2}\gg(\Delta g)^{2}
.

E.2
Variance-optimal state

E.2.1
Without displacement

Now, let us come to a different state that maximizes the prefactor in front of the variance term
\Delta g^{2}
.

Again, as an Ansatz, we use a state with zero displacement. It takes the form

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\hat{B}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{n}}{2}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{j}}{2}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}}}_{\hat{V}}\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{i}}(r)\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{j}}(r)|0\rangle=e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{n}}{2}\hat{B}\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}\hat{B}^{\dagger}}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{j}}{2}\hat{B}\hat{b}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{j}\hat{B}^{\dagger}}\hat{S}_{\hat{B}\hat{a}_{i}\hat{B}^{\dagger}}(r)\hat{S}_{\hat{B}\hat{a}_{j}\hat{B}^{\dagger}}(r)|0\rangle

(273)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{n}}{2}\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{j}}{2}\hat{b}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{j}}\hat{S}_{\hat{b}_{i}}(r)\hat{S}_{\hat{b}_{j}}(r)|0\rangle

(274)

\displaystyle=\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{i}/2}\hat{b}_{i}}(r)\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{j}/2}\hat{b}_{j}}(r)|0\rangle

(275)

Thus, we squeeze two mode in the mode basis
\{\hat{b}_{n}\}_{n}
in which the generator
\hat{G}
appears diagonal. Note that we squeeze both modes
i
and
j
equally with squeezing parameters and with squeezing angles
\phi_{i},\phi_{j}
. In the second line we used Eq. (
87
). Let us also note we assume
g_{i},g_{j}\neq 0
.

The QFI and the resources of such a state can be calculated quite easily due to the state’s simple structure. For the QFI we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}]

(276)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{ij}s_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}c_{i}+4\sum_{ij}c_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{i}

(277)

\displaystyle=8\sum_{i}g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}c_{i}^{2}=8g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}c_{i}^{2}+8g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}c_{j}^{2}

(278)

\displaystyle=8\tilde{g}_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+8g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1).

(279)

Note that in the second line we used
V=BT
, where
T_{nm}=e^{-i\frac{-i\phi_{n}}{2}}\delta_{nm}
, where
\phi_{n}=0
for
n\neq i,j
. With this, we find
\tilde{G}=V^{\dagger}GV=T^{\dagger}B^{\dagger}GBT=T^{\dagger}DT=D=\text{diag}(g_{1},\ldots,g_{M})
.

Now, we want to express the QFI in terms of the resources
N_{S}
,
\bar{g}
, and
\Delta g
. We have

\displaystyle N_{S}=\text{Tr}[\tilde{P}_{S}S^{2}]=\text{Tr}[S^{2}]=s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2},

(280)

where
\tilde{P}_{S}S^{2}=S^{2}
follows from our initial assumption
g_{i},g_{j}\neq 0
.
For the mean resource, we have

\displaystyle\bar{g}N_{S}=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}]=\sum_{nm}\tilde{G}_{nm}s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\tilde{G}_{nn}s_{n}^{2}=\tilde{G}_{ii}s_{i}^{2}+\tilde{G}_{jj}s_{j}^{2}

(281)

\displaystyle=g_{i}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}s_{j}^{2}

(282)

and for the variance resource we have

\displaystyle\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]=\sum_{nm}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nm}s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}=\sum_{n}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nn}s_{n}^{2}

(283)

\displaystyle=\sum_{rn}\tilde{G}_{nr}\tilde{G}_{rn}s_{n}^{2}=\sum_{rn}|\tilde{G}_{rn}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}

(284)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r}|\tilde{G}_{ri}|^{2}s_{i}^{2}+\sum_{r}|\tilde{G}_{rj}|^{2}s_{j}^{2}

(285)

\displaystyle=g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}

(286)

Let us now set
s_{i}^{2}=s_{j}^{2}=N_{S}/2
. From this follows

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}=\frac{g_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}}{2}

(287)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=8\left(g_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}\right)\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(\frac{N_{S}}{2}+1\right)

(288)

\displaystyle=2\cdot 2\frac{g_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}}{2}N_{S}(N_{S}+2)

(289)

\displaystyle=4\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+8\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}

(290)

\displaystyle=4\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S}).

(291)

We see that the factor
4
in front of the bandwidth term
(\Delta g)^{2}
is optimal, while the factor
4
in front of the
\bar{g}^{2}
term is suboptimal, it is only
50\%
of the optimal factor
8
. Thus, the state is variance optimal

We also make an important observation for the
N_{S}\ll 1
scenario. In this case, the performance of our state is a factor
2
better than that of the classical coherent state.

E.2.2
With displacement

Let us now come to a class of states that makes use of squeezing and displacement and does not rely on the generator-induced mode basis
\{\hat{b}_{i}\}_{i}
in contrast to the previous state in
E.2.1
. It relies on populating an initial mode and the corresponding derivative mode, and as we show here, under certain conditions this state can be variance optimal. It has been widely discussed in the literature
50
;
22
.

First recall that in an arbitrary basis
\{\hat{a}_{n}\}_{n}
we have

\partial_{\lambda}\hat{a}_{m}^{\dagger}(\lambda)=\partial_{\lambda}\hat{U}_{\lambda}\hat{a}_{m}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}=-i\sum_{n}G_{nm}\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger}(\lambda).

We see that the derivative mode
\partial_{\lambda}\hat{a}_{m}^{\dagger}
can be expressed in terms of the initial modes.

Now, let us choose an arbitrary initial mode
\hat{u}_{0}
. It is always possible to find a mode basis
\{\hat{u}_{0},\hat{u}_{1},\ldots\}
such that the derivative mode of
\hat{u}_{0}
is composed of only two modes

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\hat{u}_{0}^{\dagger}(\lambda)=-i[G_{00}\hat{u}_{0}^{\dagger}(\lambda)+G_{10}\hat{u}_{1}^{\dagger}(\lambda)].

(292)

We refer to
\hat{u}_{1}
as the derivative mode of
\hat{u}_{0}
, as it is the only mode (other than
\hat{u}_{0}
itself) that has overlap with
\partial_{\lambda}\hat{u}_{0}(\lambda)
.
Now, suppose we displace
\hat{u}_{0}
and squeeze
\hat{u}_{0}
and
\hat{u}_{1}
, that is, we consider the state

\displaystyle\hat{V}\hat{D}_{\hat{u}_{0}}(\alpha_{0})\hat{S}_{\hat{u}_{0}}(r_{0})\hat{S}_{\hat{u}_{1}}(r_{1})|0\rangle,

(293)

where
\hat{V}=e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{0}}{2}\hat{u}_{0}^{\dagger}\hat{u}_{0}}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{1}}{2}\hat{u}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{u}_{1}}
and we have
V_{ni}=e^{-i(-\phi_{n})/2}\delta_{ni}
, where
\phi_{n}
is related to the squeezing angle.

Let us calculate the QFI and the resources of such a state. (assume
G
is full rank). Recall that
\tilde{G}=V^{\dagger}GV
.
The QFI of the state is then

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=

\displaystyle 4\Big[\tilde{G}_{00}^{2}\left(|\alpha_{0}|^{2}\left(c_{0}^{2}+s_{0}^{2}\right)+c_{0}s_{0}\left(\alpha_{0}^{2}+\alpha_{0}^{*2}+2c_{0}s_{0}\right)\right)

(294)

\displaystyle+\tilde{G}_{11}^{2}\cdot 2c_{1}^{2}s_{1}^{2}

(295)

\displaystyle+|\tilde{G}_{01}|^{2}\Big((s_{1}^{2}+c_{1}^{2})|\alpha_{0}|^{2}+s_{1}^{2}c_{0}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}s_{0}^{2}

(296)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+s_{1}c_{1}(e^{2i\gamma}\{\alpha_{0}^{2}+c_{0}s_{0}\}+e^{-2i\gamma}\{\alpha_{0}^{*2}+c_{0}s_{0}\})\Big)\Big]

(297)

where we used
\tilde{G}_{01}=G_{01}e^{i\frac{\phi_{1}-\phi_{0}}{2}}
and denoted
\gamma=\frac{\phi_{1}-\phi_{0}}{2}+\arg[G_{01}]
and note that
\tilde{G}_{00}=G_{00}
and
\tilde{G}_{11}=G_{11}
. Choosing
\gamma=0
and
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
clearly maximizes the QFI, which is why we will use this parameter setting in the following.

For the resources we have

\displaystyle N_{S}=s_{0}^{2}+s_{1}^{2}+|\alpha_{0}|^{2}

(298)

\displaystyle\bar{g}N_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}\tilde{G}_{nm}(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m})

(299)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\tilde{G}_{nn}s_{n}^{2}+\sum_{n,m}\tilde{G}_{nm}\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}

(300)

\displaystyle=\tilde{G}_{00}s_{0}^{2}+\tilde{G}_{11}s_{1}^{2}+\tilde{G}_{00}|\alpha_{0}|^{2}

(301)

We also have

\displaystyle(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2})N_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nm}(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m})

(302)

\displaystyle=(\tilde{G}^{2})_{00}s_{0}^{2}+(\tilde{G}^{2})_{11}s_{1}^{2}+(\tilde{G}^{2})_{00}|\alpha_{0}|^{2}

(303)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{0i}\tilde{G}_{i0}s_{0}^{2}+\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{1i}\tilde{G}_{i1}s_{1}^{2}+\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{0i}\tilde{G}_{i0}|\alpha_{0}|^{2}

(304)

\displaystyle=\left[|\tilde{G}_{00}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{01}|^{2}\right](s_{0}^{2}+|\alpha|^{2})+\left[|\tilde{G}_{10}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{11}|^{2}\right]s_{1}^{2}

(305)

Now, assuming
s_{0}^{2}=0
,
|\alpha_{0}|^{2}=s_{1}^{2}=N_{S}/2
and considering the limit
N_{S}\gg 1
, we obtain for the resources
\bar{g}=(\tilde{G}_{00}+\tilde{G}_{11})/2
,
\Delta g^{2}=|\tilde{G}_{01}|^{2}+(\tilde{G}_{00}-\tilde{G}_{11})^{2}/4
and for the QFI

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\approx 2\tilde{G}_{11}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\left(\Delta g^{2}-(\tilde{G}_{00}-\tilde{G}_{11})^{2}\frac{1}{4}\right)N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S}).

(306)

We see that if
\tilde{G}_{00}-\tilde{G}_{11}=G_{00}-G_{11}=0
, then the state is variance optimal with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\approx 2\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S}).

(307)

The performance associated to the variance term of the QFI can be attained with a homodyne measurement of the mode
\hat{u}_{1}(\lambda)
.

Also note that this state has been discussed in the parameter setting
|\alpha_{0}|^{2}\gg s_{0}^{2}
and
|\alpha_{0}|^{2}\gg s_{1}^{2}
. With the additional assumption of
s_{0}^{2},s_{1}^{2}\gg 1
, we obtain
\mathcal{F}\approx 4\left[\bar{g}^{2}e^{2r_{0}}+\Delta g^{2}e^{2r_{1}}\right]N_{S}
.

E.3
Completely optimal state

In Sec.
III.4.1
of the main text, we identified the optimal state. We now verify that this state is indeed optimal. Recall that it takes the form

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{i}/2}\hat{b}_{i}}(r_{i})\,\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{j}/2}\hat{b}_{j}}(r_{j})\,|0\rangle,

(308)

which generalizes the variance-optimal state of Sec.
E.2
by allowing the two squeezing strengths to differ,
i.e
.,
r_{i}\neq r_{j}
.

First, recall that we assume
g_{i}<g_{j}
and
g_{i}\neq 0
and
g_{j}\neq 0
. Now, assume that
i,j
exists such that the corresponding eigenvalues are

\displaystyle g_{i}

\displaystyle=\bar{g}-|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}}

(309)

\displaystyle g_{j}

\displaystyle=\bar{g}+|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}}

(310)

and the average photon number of mode
i,j
are chosen as

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1-\frac{\bar{g}}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}}}\right)

(311)

\displaystyle s_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1+\frac{\bar{g}}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}}}\right)

(312)

where
N_{S}=s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2}
which follows from
g_{i},g_{j}\neq 0
.

The calculation for the QFI and resources is analogous to the one in the previous Sec.
E.2
and we find

\displaystyle N_{S}

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}[S^{2}]=s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2}

(313)

\displaystyle\bar{g}N_{S}

\displaystyle=g_{i}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}s_{j}^{2}

(314)

\displaystyle\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}

\displaystyle=g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}

(315)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=8g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+8g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1).

(316)

Let us first show that everything is self consistent.
We start with the mean resource:

\displaystyle\bar{g}N_{S}

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{g}-|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}}\right)s_{i}^{2}+\left(\bar{g}+|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}}\right)s_{j}^{2}

(317)

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{g}s_{i}^{2}-|\Delta g|\sqrt{s_{j}^{2}s_{i}^{2}}\right)+\left(\bar{g}s_{j}^{2}+|\Delta g|\sqrt{s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}}\right)

(318)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}\left(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2}\right)

(319)

Thus, we have shown self consistence for the mean resource.

Let us continue with the variance resource.

\displaystyle\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)N_{S}

\displaystyle=g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}=\left(\bar{g}-|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}}\right)^{2}s_{i}^{2}+\left(\bar{g}+|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}}\right)^{2}s_{j}^{2}

(320)
