\displaystyle=\left(\bar{g}^{2}-2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}}+\Delta g^{2}\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}\right)s_{i}^{2}+\left(\bar{g}^{2}+2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}}+\Delta g^{2}\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}\right)s_{j}^{2}

(321)

\displaystyle=\bar{g}\left(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2}\right)+\Delta g^{2}\left(s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2}\right).

(322)

Thus, we have shown self consistence for the variance resource.

Now, it only remains to show that the QFI reaches its optimal value. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=8g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+8g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)

(323)

\displaystyle=8\left(\bar{g}-|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}}\right)^{2}s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+8\left(\bar{g}+|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}}\right)^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)

(324)

\displaystyle=8\left(\bar{g}^{2}-2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}}+\Delta g^{2}\frac{s_{j}^{2}}{s_{i}^{2}}\right)s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+8\left(\bar{g}^{2}+2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}}+\Delta g^{2}\frac{s_{i}^{2}}{s_{j}^{2}}\right)s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)

(325)

\displaystyle=8\bar{g}^{2}\left[s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)\right]+8\Delta g^{2}\left[s_{j}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+s_{i}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)\right]+8\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}}\left[s_{j}^{2}+1-(s_{i}^{2}+1)\right]

(326)

\displaystyle=8\bar{g}^{2}\left[s_{i}^{4}+s_{j}^{4}\right]+8\cdot 2\Delta g^{2}s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}+8\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}}\left[s_{j}^{2}-s_{i}^{2}\right]+8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}+8\Delta g^{2}N_{S}+0.

(327)

To make further progress, let us consider the following terms:
Let us abbreviate
q=\frac{\bar{g}}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}}}

\displaystyle s_{i}^{4}+s_{j}^{4}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{N_{S}}{2}\right)^{2}\left(1-q\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{N_{S}}{2}\right)^{2}\left(1+q\right)^{2}

(328)

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}^{2}}{2}\left[1+q^{2}\right]

(329)

Next:

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2}s_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1-q\right)\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1+q\right)=\left(\frac{N_{S}}{2}\right)^{2}\left(1-q^{2}\right)

(330)

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}^{2}}{4}\left(1-q^{2}\right)

(331)

Lastly, let us consider

\displaystyle s_{j}^{2}-s_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1+q\right)-\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1-q\right)=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1+q-1+q\right)=\frac{N_{S}}{2}2q

(332)

\displaystyle=+N_{S}q

(333)

These results can be plugged into the QFI and we obtain:

\displaystyle=8\bar{g}^{2}\frac{N_{S}^{2}}{2}[1+q^{2}]+8\cdot 2\Delta g^{2}\frac{N_{S}^{2}}{4}(1-q^{2})+8\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|\sqrt{\frac{N_{S}^{2}}{4}(1-q^{2})}(+)N_{S}q+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}

(334)

\displaystyle=4\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}[1+q^{2}]+4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}(1-q^{2})+4\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|N_{S}^{2}\sqrt{(1-q^{2})}q+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}

(335)

\displaystyle=4\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}q^{2}-4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}q^{2}+4\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|N_{S}^{2}\sqrt{(1-q^{2})}q+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}

(336)

\displaystyle=8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}(q^{2}-1)-4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}q^{2}+4\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|N_{S}^{2}\sqrt{(1-q^{2})}q+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}

(337)

Now, the first two terms in the last line above corresponds to optimal QFI for given resources. To prove optimality, we thus have to show that the other terms containing
q
expressions cancel each other out to zero. So let us consider:

\displaystyle 4

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}(q^{2}-1)-4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}q^{2}+4\cdot 2\bar{g}|\Delta g|N_{S}^{2}\sqrt{(1-q^{2})}q

(338)

\displaystyle=-4N_{S}^{2}\left[\bar{g}\frac{|\Delta g|}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}}}-|\Delta g|\frac{\bar{g}}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+(\Delta g)^{2}}}\right]^{2}.

(339)

With this, we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}.

(340)

E.4
An idler-assisted optimal state

The optimal states we have considered thus far have not made use of an idler beam. So let us now consider states that make use of idler modes.
We consider a state with
4
populated initial modes labelled as
i,i_{I},j,j_{I}
. The state then takes the form

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\hat{B}\hat{U}_{ii_{I}}\hat{U}_{jj_{I}}}_{=\hat{V}}\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{i}}(r_{i})\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{i_{I}}}(r_{i_{I}})\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{j}}(r_{j})\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{j_{I}}}(r_{j_{I}})|0\rangle.

(341)

The unitaries
\hat{U}_{nn_{I}}
with
n=i,j
are beam splitter unitaries that act as

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{nn+2}^{\dagger}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}_{n}\\
\hat{a}_{n_{I}}\end{pmatrix}\hat{U}_{nn_{I}}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta_{nn_{I}}&-\sin\theta_{nn_{I}}\,e^{i\phi_{nn_{I}}}\\
\sin\theta_{nn_{I}}\,e^{-i\phi_{nn_{I}}}&\cos\theta_{nn_{I}}\end{pmatrix}}_{=U_{nn_{I}}}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}_{n}\\
\hat{a}_{n_{I}}\end{pmatrix},

(342)

We recall that the QFI is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}CS\tilde{G}^{*}CS]+4\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}S^{2}\tilde{G}C^{2}].

(343)

In this case, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{G}=V^{\dagger}GV=U_{jj_{I}}^{\dagger}U_{ii_{I}}^{\dagger}B^{\dagger}GBU_{ii_{I}}U_{jj_{I}}.

(344)

With this, we can easily calculate the QFI and the relevant resources. Let us make the parameter choices
r_{i}=r_{i_{I}}
,
r_{j}=r_{j_{I}}
,
\theta_{ii_{I}}=\pi/4
,
\theta_{jj_{I}}=\pi/4
. This yields two two-mode squeezed vacuum states between modes
i
and
i_{I}
and modes
j
and
J_{I}
. Let us additionally set
\phi_{ii_{I}}=0
and
\phi_{jj_{I}}=0
, this determines the squeezing angles of our two-mode squeezed vacua. With this, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=4\cdot 2g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+4\cdot 2g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)

(345)

and for the resources

\displaystyle N_{S}=\text{Tr}[\tilde{P}_{S}S^{2}\tilde{P}_{S}]=s_{i}^{2}+s_{j}^{2}

(346)

and

\displaystyle\bar{g}N_{S}=g_{i}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}s_{j}^{2}

(347)

\displaystyle(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}=\text{Tr}[\tilde{G}^{2}S^{2}]=g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}+g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}.

(348)

The structure of the QFI and the resources is exactly the same as for the idler-less optimal state in the previous Subsections
E.2
and
E.3
, compare Eqs. (
279
), (
280
),(
282
) and (
286
) with the above four equations.

Due to the fact that the structure of QFI and resources is identical for the idler-less and idler-assisted state, the discussion about optimality for our idler-assisted strategy is completely analogous to the idler-less case in Sections
E.2
and
E.3
. Thus, also the idler-assisted state presented here can be optimal (and also variance optimal) in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+4\Delta g^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S}),

(349)

given that we find appropriate modes and populate them according to our findings in
E.3
.

Appendix F
Optimal measurements

F.1
Optimal measurement for variance-optimal and completely optimal state

Let us now present an optimal measurement for the variance-optimal and the completely optimal state given in Eq. (
40
) and Eq. (
34
), respectively. Recall that the state is of the form
|\psi\rangle=\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{i}/2}\hat{b}_{i}}(r_{i})\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{j}/2}\hat{b}_{j}}(r_{j})|0\rangle
.
Now, as both modes
\hat{b}_{i}
and
\hat{b}_{j}
are orthogonal, we can homodyne both of them simultaneously. We measure

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{e^{-i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{b}_{n}}=\frac{e^{-i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{b}_{n}+e^{+i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}}{\sqrt{2}}

(350)

for
n=i,j
and
\varphi_{n}
is the adjustable phase that determines the quadrature to be measured. We have
\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\hat{x}_{e^{-i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{b}_{n}}|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle=0
and

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\hat{x}^{2}_{e^{-i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{b}_{n}}|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle=\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\frac{\hat{b}_{n}^{2}e^{-i2(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}+\hat{b}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}e^{+i2(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}+\hat{b}_{n}\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}+\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{n}}{2}|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle

(351)

\displaystyle=\langle\psi|\hat{U}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\frac{\hat{b}_{n}^{2}e^{-i2(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}+\hat{b}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}e^{+i2(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}+\hat{b}_{n}\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}+\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{n}}{2}\hat{U}_{\lambda}|\psi\rangle

(352)

\displaystyle=\langle\psi|\frac{\hat{b}_{n}^{2}e^{-i2(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n}+\lambda g_{n})}+\hat{b}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}e^{+i2(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n}+\lambda g_{n})}+\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{n}+\hat{b}_{n}\hat{b}_{n}^{\dagger}}{2}|\psi\rangle

(353)

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{n}c_{n}e^{-i2(\varphi_{n}+\lambda g_{n})}+s_{n}c_{n}e^{+i2(\varphi_{n}+\lambda g_{n})}+s_{n}^{2}+s_{n}^{2}+1}{2}

(354)

\displaystyle=s_{n}c_{n}\cos(2(\varphi_n+\lambda g_n))+\frac{2s_{n}^{2}+1}{2}

(355)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh(2r)\cos(2(\varphi_n+\lambda g_n))}{2}+\frac{\cosh(2r)}{2}.

(356)

In the third line we used
\hat{U}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{n}\hat{U}_{\lambda}=\hat{b}_{n}e^{-i\lambda g_{n}}
.

Note that due to the fact that the state is separable with respect to the measured modes
i
and
j
, the corresponding measurement outcomes are independent
x_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2})
. The FI of the joint Gaussian probability distributions is the sum of the individual mode FIs. With this, we find

\displaystyle F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}F_{\text{hom},n}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

(357)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\partial_{\lambda}\sigma_{\text{hom},n})^{2}}{\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}}=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}\frac{1}{8}\frac{\partial_{\lambda}(\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2})}{\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{4}}

(358)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}\frac{2g_{n}^{2}\sinh^{2}(2r_{n})\sin^{2}(2(g_{n}\lambda+\varphi_{n}))}{(\cosh(2 r_n)+\sinh(2 r_n)\cos(2
(g_n \lambda+\varphi_n )))^{2}}.

(359)

Now, the optimal measurement phases
\varphi_{n}^{\text{opt}}
can be easily determined
45
, and we find

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}^{\text{opt}}=\frac{1}{2}\arccos(\tanh(2 r_n))-g_{n}\lambda+\frac{\pi}{2}

(360)

with which we find the optimal FI

\displaystyle\max_{\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}}F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=8g_{i}^{2}s_{i}^{2}(s_{i}^{2}+1)+8g_{j}^{2}s_{j}^{2}(s_{j}^{2}+1)

(361)

which is identical to the QFIs in Eq. (
316
) and Eq. (
279
) of the optimal state and the variance-optimal state, from which it follows that our proposed homodyne scheme is an optimal measurement for these states.

F.2
Effect of loss and noise

Let us also consider the effect of photon loss. We model photon loss as a beam splitter of transmissivity
\eta_{n}
right before homodyne detection that mixes the signal mode with a zero-mean environmental mode in a thermal (Gaussian) state.
In that case, the mean of the homodyne measurement is still zero and the variance is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}(\eta_{n})=\eta_{n}\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}(0)+(1-\eta_{n})\sigma_{\text{env},n}^{2},

(362)

where
\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}(0)=\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}
is the ideal case.

Now, the calculations for the FI are analogous to above, and we find

\displaystyle F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}\frac{1}{8}\frac{\partial_{\lambda}(\eta_{n}\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}(0))}{\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{4}(\eta)}

(363)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}\frac{2\eta_{n}^{2}g_{n}^{2}\sinh^{2}(2r_{n})\sin^{2}(2(g_{n}\lambda+\varphi_{n}))}{(\eta_{n}\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}(0)+(1-\eta_{n})\sigma_{\text{env},n}^{2})^{2}}.

(364)

The optimal phase is given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}^{\text{opt}}=\frac{1}{2}\arccos\left(\frac{\eta_{n}\sinh(2r_n)}{\eta_{n}\cosh(2r_n)+(1-\eta_{n})\sigma^{2}_{\text{env},n}}\right)-g_{n}\lambda+\frac{\pi}{2}.

(365)

For the optimal FI under loss and thermal noise, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}}F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=\sum_{n=\{i,j\}}\frac{2\eta_{n}^{2}g_{n}^{2}\sinh^{2}(2r_{n})}{(\eta_{n}\cosh(2r_n)+(1-\eta_{n})\sigma^{2}_{\text{env},n})^{2}+\eta_{n}^{2}\sinh(2r_n))^{4}}.

(366)

We thus have obtained a general expression for the FI of an optimal homodyne measurement when loss and noise is present. To gain further insights, let us assume
\eta_{i}=\eta_{j}=:\eta
, which is, for example, approximately the case in lidar and radar scenarios.

For the case
\sigma_{\text{env},n}=1
, which corresponds to an environment in the vacuum state, and the case
\sinh^{2}(r_{n})\gg 1
for
n=\{i,j\}
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}}F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{2\eta}{1-\eta}\left[g_{i}^{2}\sinh^{2}(r_{i})+g_{j}^{2}\sinh^{2}(r_{j})\right]

(367)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\eta}{1-\eta}\left[\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}\right]N_{S},

(368)

where we used
\sinh^{2}(2r)\approx 4\sinh^{2}(r)
and
\cosh(2r)\approx 2\sinh^{2}(r)
.
This should be compared to the performance of a classical coherent state protocol, which achieves
\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}^{\text{coh}}\rangle)=4\eta\left[\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}\right]N_{S}
. We have
\max_{\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}}F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)\geq\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}^{\text{coh}}\rangle)
, and thus a quantum advantage for transmissivities
\eta\geq 1/2
. Thus, our state that is optimal for
\eta=1
and shows good resilience even when photon loss is present.

Let us now also consider the effect of noise. Assume that the environmental modes are in thermal states with average photon number
N_{B}
. We then have
\sigma_{\text{env},n}=2N_{B}/(1-\eta)+1
(the factor
1/(1-\eta)
ensures that we have the same thermal noise photon number for all
\eta
). Assume
N_{B}\gg 1
to be such that
\sigma_{\text{env},n}\approx 2N_{B}/(1-\eta)
. We then find

\displaystyle\max_{\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}}F_{\text{hom}}(\Delta\lambda=0)=8\eta^{2}\sum_{n=n_{0},n_{1}}\frac{g_{n}^{2}\sinh^{2}(r_{n})}{8\eta\sinh^{2}(r_{n})N_{B}+4N_{B}^{2}}

(369)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\eta}{N_{B}}\left[g_{i}^{2}\sinh^{2}(r_{i})+g_{n_{1}}^{2}\sinh^{2}(r_{j})\right]=\frac{\eta N_{S}}{N_{B}}\left[\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}\right],\quad\text{for }\eta\sinh^{2}(r_{n})\gg N_{B}\\
2\eta^{2}\left[g_{i}^{2}\frac{\sinh^{4}(r_{i})}{N_{B}^{2}}+g_{j}^{2}\frac{\sinh^{4}(r_{j})}{N_{B}^{2}}\right],\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{for }\eta\sinh^{2}(r_{n})\ll N_{B}\end{cases}

(370)

with
n=\{i,j\}
.

The upper case is a factor of
2
lower than the classical coherent state performance
\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)\approx 2\frac{\eta N_{S}}{N_{B}}\left[\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}\right]
. The performance in the second case is much worse as the FI scales as
\sim(\frac{\eta N_{S}}{N_{B}})^{2}\left[\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}\right]
with
\eta N_{S}/N_{B}\ll 1
. Thus, our state that is otherwise optimal for
\eta=1
and
N_{B}=0
, is inferior in the case of
\eta<1
and
N_{B}\gg 1
to the classical coherent state.

F.3
Optimal homodyne measurement for mean-optimal state

Let us now propose an optimal measurement for the mean-optimal single-mode squeezed state
|\psi\rangle=\hat{V}\hat{S}_{\hat{a}_{n}}(r_{n})|0\rangle=\hat{S}_{\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}}(r_{n})|0\rangle
. Let us assume we have a sufficiently good prior guess
\lambda_{\text{prior}}
of the parameter
\lambda
. With out loss of generality, less us assume this prior guess is
\lambda_{\text{prior}}=0
and
\lambda\approx 0
.
We then have
\hat{U}_{\lambda}\simeq\hat{\mathbb{1}}-i\lambda\hat{G}
.

Now, we propose to homodyne the mode
\hat{c}_{n}=\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}
, which corresponds to measuring the operator

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{\hat{c}_{n}e^{-i\varphi_{n}}}=\frac{\hat{c}_{i}e^{-i\varphi_{n}}+\hat{c}_{i}^{\dagger}e^{+i\varphi_{n}}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(371)

We find
\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\hat{x}_{e^{-i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{c}_{n}}|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle=0
and

\displaystyle\sigma_{\text{hom},n}^{2}

\displaystyle:=\langle\psi_{\lambda}|\hat{x}^{2}_{e^{-i(\phi_{n}/2+\varphi_{n})}\hat{b}_{n}}|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle\approx\frac{\sinh(2r)\cos(2(\varphi_n+\tilde G_{nn}))}{2}+\frac{\cosh(2r)}{2}.

(372)

Here, the approximation sign means that we neglect terms of order
\lambda^{2}
and higher. Analogously to the previous Sec.
F.1
, we find

\displaystyle\max_{\varphi_{n}}F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=8\tilde{G}_{nn}^{2}s_{n}^{2}(s_{n}^{2}+1)=8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S},

(373)

where the expression in terms of resources after the last equals sign follows from our results in Sec.
E.1
. Compare this to the QFI of this state
\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda})=8\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+8(\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2})N_{S}
.
We see that the single homodyne measurement we proposed is only sensitive to the mean resource
\bar{g}
, and not the the variance resource
\Delta g
. In the limit
N_{S}\gg 1
, the single-mode homodyne measurement is nearly optimal.

Appendix G
Comparison to previous approaches in the literature

In Sec.
III.6
we discussed the approach of Ref.
27
for (optimal) states that maximize the QFI for a parameter imprinting unitary
\hat{U}_{\lambda}=e^{-i\lambda\hat{G}}
in a finite dimensional Hilbert space, where
\hat{G}
is a finite dimensional Hermitian operator. Their results do not apply to our case of mode parameter estimation as in that case we are dealing with an infinite dimensional Hilbert space.

To compare their approach with the one presented here, let us introduce a photon number truncation to turn the infinitely dimensional Hilbert space encountered in mode parameter estimation into a finite one:

\displaystyle\hat{G}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}g_{i}\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}g_{i}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n|n\rangle_{i}\langle n|\rightarrow\sum_{i=1}^{M}g_{i}\sum_{n=0}^{N_{\text{cut}}}n|n\rangle_{i}\langle n|

(374)

where
|n\rangle_{i}=\hat{b}_{i}^{{\dagger}n}|0\rangle/\sqrt{n!}
are Fock states of mode
i
. Without loss of generality, assume that
g_{1}=g_{\text{min}}
and
g_{M}=g_{\text{max}}
.
Thus, the initial infinite Hilbert space has been truncated to a finite dimensional one, which lets us apply the results of Ref.
27
. According to Ref.
27
, the (optimal) state that maximizes the QFI takes the form

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=\frac{|N_{\text{cut}}\rangle_{1}+|N_{\text{cut}}\rangle_{M}}{\sqrt{2}}

(375)

which is a superposition of the generator’s eigenstates corresponding to the minimal and maximal eigenvalues.

Ref.
27
did not introduce the notion of resources, but let us calculate the resources of this state according to our formalism and the express the QFI in terms of these.
We find
N_{S}=\sum_{i}\langle\psi|\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}|\psi\rangle=N_{\text{cut}}
for the signal photon number and

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\psi|\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}|\psi\rangle}{N_{S}}=\frac{\delta_{i,1}+\delta_{i,M}}{2}.

(376)

for the generator intensity. Note that this state has the same generator intensity distribution
\frac{\langle\psi|\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}|\psi\rangle}{N_{S}}
as our Gaussian variance-optimal state.
We further find

\displaystyle\bar{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}g_{i}^{2}\frac{\langle\psi|\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}|\psi\rangle}{N_{S}}=\frac{g_{\text{min}}+g_{\text{max}}}{2}

(377)

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}g_{i}^{2}\frac{\langle\psi|\hat{b}_{i}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{i}|\psi\rangle}{N_{S}}=\frac{g_{\text{min}}^{2}+g_{\text{max}}^{2}}{2}.

(378)

From this follows
\Delta g^{2}=\frac{1}{4}(g_{M}-g_{1})^{2}
. Now, from Ref.
27
we know that
\mathcal{F}(\hat{U}_{\lambda}|\psi\rangle)=(g_{\text{max}}-g_{\text{min}})^{2}N_{\text{cut}}^{2}
, from which follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\hat{U}_{\lambda}|\psi\rangle)=4\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}.

(379)

Thus, the optimal state proposed in Ref.
27
when applied to truncated mode parameter estimation is variance optimal. There appear no mean contribution
\sim\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}
in the . Thus, their state is suboptimal according to our framework.

Appendix H
Continuous mode bases

In the main text, we encountered continuous mode bases
\hat{D}(z)
satisfying the commutation relation
[\hat{D}(z),\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})]=\delta(z-z^{\prime})
. We keep the discussion general and do not assign a specific physical meaning to
\hat{D}(z)
: the variable
z\in\mathbb{R}
may represent time, frequency, a spatial coordinate, momentum
k
, or any other continuous degree of freedom.

These continuous mode operators can be expanded in terms of discrete ones as

\displaystyle\hat{D}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{d}_{n}\,\Psi_{n}(z),

(380)

where
\Psi_{n}(z)
are the mode functions associated with the discrete basis
\{\hat{d}_{n}\}_{n}
satisfying
[\hat{d}_{n},\hat{d}_{m}^{\dagger}]=\delta_{nm}

3
. The mode functions form a complete orthonormal set,
\int\mathrm{d}z\,\psi_{n}^{*}(z)\,\psi_{m}(z)=\delta_{nm}
, allowing one to pass freely between a continuous and a discrete mode description. Note, however, that the discrete description still involves countably infinitely many modes.

A second important continuous mode basis is
\hat{F}(p)
, with
p\in\mathbb{R}
, related to
\hat{D}(z)
by a Fourier transform:

\displaystyle\hat{D}(z)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}p\,\hat{F}(p)\,\frac{e^{-ipz}}{\sqrt{2\pi}},

(381)

\displaystyle\hat{F}(p)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\hat{D}(z)\,\frac{e^{+ipz}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}.

(382)

A multimode squeezed state may be written equivalently in any of these three bases as

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\exp\!\left(\iint\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,f(z,z^{\prime})\,\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)|0\rangle,

(383)

\displaystyle=\exp\!\left(\iint\mathrm{d}p\,\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\,\tilde{f}(p,p^{\prime})\,\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p^{\prime})-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)|0\rangle,

(384)

\displaystyle=\exp\!\left(\sum_{n,m}f^{(\psi)}_{nm}\,\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{d}_{m}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)|0\rangle,

(385)

with
\tilde{f}(p,p^{\prime})=\iint\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,f(z,z^{\prime})\,e^{ipz}e^{ip^{\prime}z^{\prime}}/(2\pi)
and
f^{(\psi)}_{nm}=\iint\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\,f(z,z^{\prime})\,\psi_{n}^{*}(z)\,\psi_{m}^{*}(z^{\prime})
.

The shift transformations are of particular interest and are studied extensively here. The corresponding unitaries are
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=e^{-ia_{z}\hat{G}_{a_{z}}}
and
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}=e^{-ia_{p}\hat{G}_{a_{p}}}
, with generators

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{a_{z}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}p\,p\,\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p),

(386)

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{a_{p}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}z\,z\,\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z).

(387)

Let us now examine how states transform under these unitaries. One verifies directly that
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{F}(p)\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=\hat{F}(p)\,e^{-ipa_{z}}
and
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{a_{p}}=\hat{D}(z)\,e^{-iza_{p}}
. Combining these with Eqs. (
381
)–(
382
) yields
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=\hat{D}(z+a_{z})
and
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{F}(p)\hat{U}_{a_{p}}=\hat{F}(p-a_{p})
. Using
\hat{U}_{-a_{z}}=\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}
and
\hat{U}_{-a_{p}}=\hat{U}_{a_{p}}^{\dagger}
, it further follows that
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}=\hat{D}(z-a_{z})
and
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}\hat{F}(p)\hat{U}_{a_{p}}^{\dagger}=\hat{F}(p+a_{p})
. Finally, applying these results and performing a change of integration variables, one finds that
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}
acting on
|\psi^{\text{sq}}\rangle
induces
f(z,z^{\prime})\to f(z+a_{z},z^{\prime}+a_{z})
, while
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}
induces
\tilde{f}(p,p^{\prime})\to\tilde{f}(p-a_{p},p^{\prime}-a_{p})
.

H.1
Procedure to reduce infinitely many modes to finitely many modes

As discussed in the main text, we frequently encounter situations involving infinitely many modes, whereas our formalism was developed for a finite number of modes. We now demonstrate how the formalism can nevertheless be applied in this setting.

Since experimental constraints confine the relevant range of the variable
z
to a finite interval
[z_{\text{min}},z_{\text{max}}]
, we partition this interval into
M
equally sized bins of width
\delta z:=(z_{\text{max}}-z_{\text{min}})/M
and define the discretized annihilation operators

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{n}:=\int_{z_{\text{min}}+\delta z\,(n-1)}^{z_{\text{min}}+\delta z\,n}\mathrm{d}z\,\frac{\hat{D}(z)}{\sqrt{\delta z}},\qquad n\in\{1,\ldots,M\},

(388)

which satisfy
[\hat{d}_{n},\hat{d}_{m}^{\dagger}]=\delta_{nm}
, yielding a finite set of
M
modes with the desired commutation relations.

Discretized versions of
\hat{F}(p)
are obtained as follows. The
p
-interval
[p_{\text{min}},p_{\text{max}}]
has length
p_{\text{max}}-p_{\text{min}}=2\pi/\delta z=2\pi M/(z_{\text{max}}-z_{\text{min}})
, with bin size
\delta p=2\pi/(z_{\text{max}}-z_{\text{min}})
. Setting
p_{k}=p_{\text{min}}+(k-1)\,\delta p
for
k\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, we define the discrete modes
\{\hat{f}_{k}\}_{k}
via the discrete Fourier relations

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\hat{f}_{k}\,\frac{e^{-iz_{n}p_{k}}}{\sqrt{M}},

(389)

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{M}\hat{d}_{n}\,\frac{e^{+iz_{n}p_{k}}}{\sqrt{M}},

(390)

which are the discrete analogues of Eqs. (
381
)–(
382
), with
z_{n}=z_{\text{min}}+n\,\delta z
. The transformation matrix
e^{-iz_{n}p_{k}}/\sqrt{M}
is unitary, as can be seen by writing

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-iz_{n}p_{k}}}{\sqrt{M}}=\frac{e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{M}(n-1)(k-1)}}{\sqrt{M}}\,e^{-i\left(z_{\min}p_{\min}+z_{\min}\delta p\,(k-1)+p_{\min}\delta z\,(n-1)\right)}.

(391)

The first factor is the standard
M
-point discrete Fourier transform matrix, which is unitary. The remaining exponential factors depend on at most one index, or are global phases, and therefore correspond to multiplication by diagonal unitary matrices on the rows and columns together with an overall phase — operations that preserve unitarity. It follows that
[\hat{f}_{k},\hat{f}_{q}^{\dagger}]=\delta_{kq}
.

For the shift transformations studied here, the continuous generators of Eqs. (
386
)–(
387
) are approximated by

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{a_{z}}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{n=1}^{M}p_{n}\,\hat{f}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{f}_{n},

(392)

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{a_{p}}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{n=1}^{M}z_{n}\,\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{d}_{n}.

(393)

The quality of this approximation improves with increasing
M
and decreasing bin width, both of which can be adjusted freely. Correspondingly, the multimode squeezed state of Eq. (
383
) is well approximated in the discretized basis as

\displaystyle|\psi^{\text{sq}}\rangle

\displaystyle\approx\exp\!\left(\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}f_{nm}\,\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{d}_{m}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)|0\rangle,

(394)

\displaystyle\approx\exp\!\left(\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}\tilde{f}_{nm}\,\hat{f}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{f}_{m}^{\dagger}-\mathrm{h.c.}\right)|0\rangle,

(395)

with
f_{nm}=f(z_{n},z_{m})
and
\tilde{f}_{nm}=\tilde{f}(p_{n},p_{m})
. This procedure reduces an infinite continuous model to a finite discrete one, justifying the application of our formalism to continuous mode settings.

H.2
Determining the generator matrix

Given only the mode functions
\psi_{n}(z;\lambda)
, it is not immediately clear how to recover the corresponding unitary mode transformation
\hat{U}_{\lambda}
. However, the generator
\hat{G}=\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}G_{nm}\,\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{m}
can be computed directly from the mode functions, and this is sufficient to evaluate the QFI.

To this end, we define

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{n}(\lambda):=\hat{U}_{\lambda}\,\hat{d}_{n}\,\hat{U}_{\lambda}^{\dagger}

(396)

as the parameter-dependent mode operators in the Schrödinger picture, in contrast to the Heisenberg picture transformation rules of Eq. (
3
). Following Ref.
23
, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\partial_{\lambda}\hat{d}_{m}^{\dagger}(\lambda)\,\hat{d}_{m}(\lambda)\,|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle

(397)

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{n,m=1}^{M}G_{nm}\,\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}(\lambda)\,\hat{d}_{m}(\lambda)\,|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle,

(398)

from which it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\hat{d}_{m}^{\dagger}(\lambda)=-i\sum_{n=1}^{M}G_{nm}\,\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}(\lambda).

(399)

Using
\partial_{\lambda}\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}(\lambda)=\int\mathrm{d}z\,[\partial_{\lambda}\Psi_{n}(z;\lambda)]\,\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)
, this yields the key relation

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\Psi_{m}(z;\lambda)=-i\sum_{n=1}^{M}G_{nm}\,\Psi_{n}(z;\lambda).

(400)

In practice,
\partial_{\lambda}\Psi_{n}(z;\lambda)
often satisfies convenient recurrence relations that allow
G_{nm}
to be computed straightforwardly. The generator matrix elements can also be obtained directly via

\displaystyle-iG_{nm}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Psi_{n}^{*}(z;\lambda)\,\partial_{\lambda}\Psi_{m}(z;\lambda).

(401)

We now apply this framework to the Hermite–Gauss (HG) modes, which form a complete orthonormal basis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. They are defined as

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(z;\,z_{0},p_{0},\sigma_{z},\theta)=\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}\pi}\right)^{1/4}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}}\,H_{n}\!\left(\frac{z-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{z}}\right)e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{z-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}e^{-ip_{0}(z-z_{0})}\,e^{-i\theta}.

(402)

Consider the mode transformation

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=\exp\!\left(-ia_{z}\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\,\hat{D}^{\dagger}(p)\,\hat{D}(p)\right),

(403)

which generates a shift in the
z
domain and acts on the mode operators as

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\,\hat{D}(p)\,\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=\hat{D}(p)\,e^{-ipa_{z}},

(404)

from which it follows that
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\,\hat{D}(z)\,\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=\hat{D}(z+a_{z})
. The transformed HG mode operators are then

\displaystyle\hat{d}_{n}(a_{z})=\hat{U}_{a_{z}}\,\hat{d}_{n}\,\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}^{*}(z)\,\hat{U}_{a_{z}}\,\hat{D}(z)\,\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}

(405)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}^{*}(z)\,\hat{D}(z-a_{z})

(406)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}^{*}(z+a_{z})\,\hat{D}(z),

(407)

and correspondingly
\hat{d}_{n}^{\dagger}(a_{z})=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}(z+a_{z})\,\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)
, so that the mode functions transform as
\Phi_{n}(z)\to\Phi_{n}(z+a_{z})
, i.e., by a simple shift. Differentiating with respect to
a_{z}
and using the recurrence relation for HG modes gives

\displaystyle\partial_{a_{z}}\Phi_{m}(z+a_{z})=\Phi_{m}^{\prime}(z+a_{z})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{z}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\,\Phi_{m-1}(z+a_{z})-\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{2}}\,\Phi_{m+1}(z+a_{z})\right)-ip_{0}\,\Phi_{m}(z+a_{z}).

(408)

Comparing with Eq. (
400
), the generator matrix elements are

\displaystyle-iG_{nm}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{z}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\,\delta_{n,m-1}-\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{2}}\,\delta_{n,m+1}\right)-ip_{0}\,\delta_{n,m},

(409)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad G_{nm}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\,\sigma_{z}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\,\delta_{n,m-1}-\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{2}}\,\delta_{n,m+1}\right)+p_{0}\,\delta_{n,m}.

(410)

H.3
QFI for a state that appears in product form in the HG mode basis

Let us consider a state that takes on a simple form in the HG mode basis

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=[\otimes_{n=1}^{M}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{n}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{n}}\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{n}}(r_{n})]|0\rangle

(411)

with
\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)
.
The states squeezing matrix is diagonal in the HG mode basis.
Note that
\hat{V}=\otimes_{n}e^{-i\frac{-\phi_{n}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}\hat{c}_{n}}
, where
\phi_{n}
is related to the squeezing angle of mode
n
. We can thus rewrite the state in various ways

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{V}[\otimes_{n}\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{n}}(r_{n})]|0\rangle=[\otimes_{n}\hat{S}_{\hat{V}\hat{c}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}}(r_{n})]|0\rangle

(412)

\displaystyle=[\otimes_{n}\hat{S}_{e^{-i\phi_{n}/2}\hat{c}_{n}}(r_{n})]|0\rangle,

(413)

which follows from
\hat{V}\hat{a}_{n}\hat{V}^{\dagger}=\sum_{i}V^{*}_{ni}\hat{a}_{i}
with
V_{ni}=e^{-i(-\phi_{n})/2}\delta_{ni}
.

Let us now calculate the QFI for
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi\rangle=|\psi_{a_{z}}\rangle
. We have

\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{a_{z}}\rangle)=4\sum_{ij}s_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}^{*}c_{i}+4\sum_{ij}c_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{i}

(414)

For simplicity, we assume that only the first two modes are populated, that is,
s_{n}=0
and
c_{n}=1
for all
n\neq 0,1
. Using Eq. (
410
) and
\tilde{G}=V^{\dagger}GV
, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{a_{z}}\rangle)=\frac{8k^{2}\sigma^{2}\left(c_{0}^{2}s_{0}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}s_{1}^{2}\right)+\left(c_{0}^{2}+2\right)s_{1}^{2}-2c_{0}c_{1}s_{0}s_{1}\cos(\phi_0 -\phi_1)+c_{1}^{2}s_{0}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}.

(415)

Let us also calculate the resource parameters. We have

\displaystyle N_{S}=s_{0}^{2}+s_{1}^{2}.

(416)

\displaystyle\bar{g}=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}\tilde{G}_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)=+p_{0}\frac{s_{0}^{2}+s_{1}^{2}}{s_{0}^{2}+s_{1}^{2}}=+p_{0}

(417)

and

\displaystyle\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)

(418)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ni}\tilde{G}_{im}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)

(419)

so that

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ni}\tilde{G}_{im}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)-\bar{g}^{2}

(420)

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{0}^{2}+3s_{1}^{2}}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}\left(s_{0}^{2}+s_{1}^{2}\right)}.

(421)

For simplicity, let us assume that
s_{0}=s_{1}
. We then have

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{2}=p_{0}^{2}

(422)

and

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}=\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}}.

(423)

For the QFI, we find with
\phi_{0}-\phi_{1}=\pi
(these phases clearly maximize the QFI):

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=2N_{S}\left(2\bar{g}^{2}(N_{S}+2)+\Delta g^{2}(N_{S}+3)\right)

(424)

\displaystyle=\left(4\bar{g}^{2}+2\Delta g^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+\underbrace{\left(8\bar{g}^{2}+6\Delta g^{2}\right)N_{S}}_{=O(N_{S})}.

(425)

We see that this state is suboptimal. Both prefactors of the mean and variance terms are a factor of
1/2
worse than the maximum possible value.

This example demonstrates how to calculate the QFI and the resource parameters for a generic state. This state is suboptimal as seen by the prefactors
4
and
2
instead of the maximum values
8
and
4
.

Appendix I
Analytical calculation of QFI for a regularized optimal state

Let us calculate the QFI of a regularized state. In the main text in Sec.
IV.3.3
we introduced such a regularized state for time estimation with squeezing matrix given in Eq. (
65
).
In this Appendix, however, we are going to be more general and instead consider the estimation of shifts
a_{z}
in the
z
domain induced by the unitary mode transform
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=e^{-a_{z}i\hat{G}_{a_{z}}}
. Here,
z
is a general variable that could stand for time, frequency, space,
k
space, etc.

Let us consider a squeezing matrix as in Eq. (
65
) in the
z
and
p
domains:

\displaystyle f(z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r_{-}\Phi_{-,0}(z)\Phi_{-,0}(z^{\prime})+r_{+}\Phi_{+,0}(z)\Phi_{+,0}(z^{\prime})

(426)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(p,p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r_{-}\tilde{\Phi}_{-,0}(p)\tilde{\Phi}_{-,0}(p^{\prime})+r_{+}\tilde{\Phi}_{+,0}(p)\tilde{\Phi}_{+,0}(p^{\prime})

(427)

where we denote

\displaystyle\Phi_{\pm,n}(z):=\Phi_{n}(z;z_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,z},p_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,p},\sigma_{z},\theta)

(428)

with
\delta_{\pm,k}\geq 0
and
\delta_{\pm,p}\geq 0
. Thus, the HG mode functions
\Phi_{\pm,n}
(defined in Eq. (
62
)) are centered around
z_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,z}
in
z
space and centered around
p_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,p}
in
p
space. Also, from Eq. (
35
) and (
36
) we know that
r_{-}\leq r_{+}
for optimal states. Let us here additionally assume the stricter
r_{-}<r_{+}
so that the case
r_{-}\neq r_{+}
is excluded.

Now, to compute the QFI given in Eq. (
17
) of the above state for the mode transform with generator
\hat{G}_{a_{z}}=\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p)
, we have to determine the components of the generator matrix
\tilde{G}=V^{\dagger}GV
in the Schmidt mode basis, that is, the basis in which the state appears disentangled.

So let us first determine the Schmidt decomposition of
f(z,z^{\prime})
. First we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{+,0}^{*}(z)\Phi_{-,0}(z).

(429)

Now, let us introduce the orthonormal vectors

\displaystyle u_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{+,0}(z)

(430)

\displaystyle u_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Phi_{-,0}(z)-|\mathcal{S}|\Phi_{+,0}(z)}{\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}}.

(431)

We can invert this and obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{+,0}(z)

\displaystyle=u_{1}(z)

(432)

\displaystyle\Phi_{-,0}(z)

\displaystyle=u_{2}(z)\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}+|\mathcal{S}|u_{1}(z).

(433)

We can now rewrite
f(z,z^{\prime})
in the orthonormal basis

\displaystyle f(z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=r_{-}\left[u_{2}(z)\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}+|\mathcal{S}|u_{1}(z)\right]\left[u_{2}(z^{\prime})\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}+|\mathcal{S}|u_{1}(z^{\prime})\right]+r_{+}u_{1}(z)u_{1}(z^{\prime})

(434)

\displaystyle=\left(r_{+}+r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\right)u_{1}(z)u_{1}(z^{\prime})+r_{-}\left(1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\right)u_{2}(z)u_{2}(z^{\prime})

(435)

\displaystyle\quad+r_{-}\left(|\mathcal{S}|\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}\right)u_{1}(z)u_{2}(z^{\prime})+r_{-}\left(|\mathcal{S}|\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}\right)u_{1}(z^{\prime})u_{2}(z)

(436)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(z^{\prime})\\
u_{2}(z^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}^{T}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}r_{+}+r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}&r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}\\
r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}&r_{-}\left(1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\right)\end{pmatrix}}_{=A}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(z)\\
u_{2}(z)\end{pmatrix}.

(437)

It is straightforward to show that the matrix’
A
eigenvalues are

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(r_{-}+r_{+}+\sqrt{4r_{-}r_{+}S^{2}+(r_{+}-r_{-})^{2}}\right)

(438)

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(r_{-}+r_{+}-\sqrt{4r_{-}r_{+}S^{2}+(r_{+}-r_{-})^{2}}\right).

(439)

Note that in the limit
\mathcal{S}\rightarrow 0
, we have
r_{1}\rightarrow r_{+}
and
r_{2}\rightarrow r_{-}
as expected, which follows from our initial assumption
r_{+}>r_{-}
.

Note that
A
is real symmetric, and thus it can be diagonalized by the orthogonal matrix

\displaystyle Q=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\chi&-\sin\chi\\
\sin\chi&\cos\chi\end{pmatrix},

(440)

that is
Q^{T}AQ=\text{diag}(r_{1},r_{2})
. For
Q^{T}AQ
to be diagonal, we get the following condition:

\displaystyle 0\overset{!}{=}(Q^{T}AQ)_{12}

\displaystyle=\cos(\chi)\sin(\chi)(A_{22}-A_{11})+A_{12}(\cos(\chi)-\sin(\chi))(\cos(\chi)+\sin(\chi))

(441)

\displaystyle=\cos(\chi)\sin(\chi)(A_{22}-A_{11})+A_{12}(\cos^{2}(\chi)-\sin^{2}(\chi))

(442)

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\tan(2\chi)

\displaystyle=-2\frac{A_{12}}{A_{22}-A_{11}}

(443)

\displaystyle=-2\frac{r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}}{r_{-}\left(1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\right)-\left[r_{+}+r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}\right]}

(444)

\displaystyle=2\frac{r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|\sqrt{1-|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}}{r_{+}-r_{-}+2r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}}

(445)

where we used
\cos^{2}(\chi)-\sin^{2}(\chi)=\cos(2\chi)
and
\cos(\chi)\sin(\chi)=\frac{1}{2}\sin(2\chi)
and assumed
A_{22}-A_{11}\neq 0
.

Now, there are two regimes: 1)
|r_{+}-r_{-}|\ll 2r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}
. If we additionally assume
|S|\ll 1
, we find
\chi=\pi/2
. 2)
|r_{+}-r_{-}|\gg 2r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}
. If we additionally assume
|S|\ll 1
, we find
\tan(2\chi)\approx 2\frac{r_{-}|S|}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\approx 0
from which follows
\chi\approx\frac{r_{-}|S|}{r_{+}-r_{-}}
up to first order in
|\mathcal{S}|
.

It is now evident that the first two Schmidt modes
\Psi_{1}(z)
and
\Psi_{2}(z)
with non-zero Schmidt coefficients defined as

\displaystyle f(z,z^{\prime})=\sum_{n=1}^{2}r_{n}\Psi_{n}(z)\Psi_{n}(z^{\prime})

(446)

are given by

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{1}(z)\\
\Psi_{2}(z)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\chi&-\sin\chi\\
\sin\chi&\cos\chi\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{1}(z)\\
u_{2}(z)\end{pmatrix}.

(447)

We will exclusively consider the regime in which
|\mathcal{S}|
is sufficiently small so that

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\cos(\chi)u_{1}(z)-\sin(\chi)u_{2}(z)\approx\cos(\chi)\Phi_{+,0}(z)-\sin(\chi)\Phi_{-,0}(z)

(448)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\cos(\chi)u_{2}(z)+\sin(\chi)u_{1}(z)\approx\cos(\chi)\Phi_{-,0}(z)+\sin(\chi)\Phi_{+,0}(z)

(449)

is a good approximation.

These are just the first two elements of the Schmidt mode basis. The rest of the elements could be constructed via a Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization procedure of the set
\{\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Phi_{+,1},\Phi_{-,1},\Phi_{+,2},\Phi_{-,2},\ldots\}
. With similar arguments as before, we find for the third and fourth element

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(z)

\displaystyle\approx\Phi_{+,1}(z)

(450)

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}(z)

\displaystyle\approx\Phi_{-,1}(z)

(451)

with negligible corrections of order
|S|
.

The next step is to determine the components of the generator matrix
\tilde{G}
in the Schmidt mode basis
\{\Psi_{n}\}_{n}
. Let us first denote

\displaystyle G_{nm}^{\Phi_{\pm}}

\displaystyle=+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{m}{2}}\delta_{n,m-1}-\sqrt{\frac{m+1}{2}}\delta_{n,m+1}\right)+(p_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,p})\delta_{n,m}

(452)

as the generator matrix in the mode bases
\{\Phi_{\pm,n}(z)\}_{n}
.

We then find

\displaystyle\partial_{a_{z}}\Psi_{1}(z+a_{z})\approx\cos(\chi)\partial_{a_{z}}\Phi_{+,0}(z+a_{z})-\sin(\chi)\partial_{a_{z}}\Phi_{-,0}(z+a_{z})

(453)

\displaystyle=\cos(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\phi_{+}}\Phi_{+,0}-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}\Phi_{+,1}\right]-\sin(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\Phi_{-,0}-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}\Phi_{-,1}\right]

(454)

\displaystyle\approx\cos(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}(\cos(\chi)\Psi_{1}+\sin(\chi)\Psi_{2})-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}\Psi_{3}\right]-\sin(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}(\cos(\chi)\Psi_{2}-\sin(\chi)\Psi_{1})-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}\Psi_{4}\right]

(455)

\displaystyle=-i\underbrace{\left[G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}\cos^{2}(\chi)+G_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\sin^{2}(\chi)\right]}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{11}}\Psi_{1}-i\underbrace{\left[G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}-G_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\right]\cos(\chi)\sin(\chi)}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{21}}\Psi_{2}-i\underbrace{G_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}\cos(\chi)}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{31}}\Psi_{3}-i\underbrace{G_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}(-\sin(\chi))}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{41}}\Psi_{4},

(456)

where we used
\Phi_{+,0}\approx\cos(\chi)\Psi_{1}+\sin(\chi)\Psi_{2}
and
\Phi_{-,0}\approx\cos(\chi)\Psi_{2}-\sin(\chi)\Psi_{1}
, which follows from Eqs. (
448
) and (
449
) by inversion, and Eq. (
400
) to determine elements of
\tilde{G}
.
Completely analogously, we find

\displaystyle\partial_{a_{z}}\Psi_{2}(z+a_{z})\approx\cos(\chi)\partial_{a_{z}}\Phi_{-,0}(z+a_{z})+\sin(\chi)\partial_{a_{z}}\Phi_{+,0}(z+a_{z})

(457)

\displaystyle=\cos(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\Phi_{-,0}-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}\Phi_{-,1}\right]+\sin(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\phi_{+}}\Phi_{+,0}-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}\Phi_{+,1}\right]

(458)

\displaystyle\approx\cos(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\left[\cos(\chi)\Psi_{2}-\sin(\chi)\Psi_{1}\right]-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}\Psi_{4}\right]+\sin(\chi)\left[-iG_{00}^{\phi_{+}}\left[\cos(\chi)\Psi_{1}+\sin(\chi)\Psi_{2}\right]-iG_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}\Psi_{3}\right]

(459)

\displaystyle=-i\underbrace{\left[G_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\cos^{2}(\chi)+G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}\sin^{2}(\chi)\right]}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{22}}\Psi_{2}-i\underbrace{\left[G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}-G_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}\right]\cos(\chi)\sin(\chi)}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{12}}\Psi_{1}-i\underbrace{G_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}\cos(\chi)}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{42}}\Psi_{4}-i\underbrace{G_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}\sin(\chi)}_{\approx\tilde{G}_{32}}\Psi_{3}

(460)

From above, we can read off the matrix elements of
\tilde{G}
.

Now, let us calculate the QFI. We use the fact that only the two modes
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are populated, and thus
s_{n}=0
and
c_{n}=1
for
n\neq 1,2
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}s_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}c_{j}s_{j}(\tilde{G}_{ji})^{*}c_{i}+4\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}c_{i}\tilde{G}_{ij}s_{j}s_{j}\tilde{G}_{ji}c_{i}

(461)

\displaystyle=4\left[2|G_{11}|^{2}s_{1}^{4}+2|G_{22}|^{2}s_{2}^{4}+2s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2}(|G_{12}|^{2}+\text{Re}[G_{12}^{2}])\right]+O(N_{S})

(462)

\displaystyle=8\left[|G_{11}|^{2}s_{1}^{4}+|G_{22}|^{2}s_{2}^{4}+s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2}(|G_{12}|^{2}+\text{Re}[G_{12}^{2}])\right]+O(N_{S}).

(463)

Let us now also calculate the resource parameters and then express the QFI in terms of these. We have

\displaystyle N_{S}=s_{1}^{2}+s_{2}^{2}

(464)

and

\displaystyle\bar{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}\tilde{G}_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)=\frac{\tilde{G}_{11}s_{1}^{2}+\tilde{G}_{22}s_{2}^{2}}{s_{1}^{2}+s_{2}^{2}}.

(465)

For the variance term, we have

\displaystyle\left(\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}(\tilde{G}^{2})_{nm}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)

(466)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{nm}\sum_{i}\tilde{G}_{ni}\tilde{G}_{im}\left(s_{n}^{2}\delta_{nm}+\alpha_{n}^{*}\alpha_{m}\right)

(467)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{n=1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tilde{G}_{ni}\tilde{G}_{in}s_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{n=1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|\tilde{G}_{ni}|^{2}s_{n}^{2}

(468)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left[|\tilde{G}_{1i}|^{2}s_{1}^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{2i}|^{2}s_{2}^{2}\right]

(469)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\left[\left(|\tilde{G}_{11}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{12}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{13}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{14}|^{2}\right)s_{1}^{2}+\left(|\tilde{G}_{21}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{22}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{23}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{24}|^{2}\right)s_{2}^{2}\right].

(470)

I.0.1
QFI of fully optimal regularized state

Above, we have given the calculation of the QFI of state with squeezing matrix as given in Eq. (
426
).

Let us now evaluate the QFI of a fully optimal state for which we generally have
r_{1}\neq r_{2}
. Let us assume we are in regime 2) with
|r_{+}-r_{-}|\gg 2r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}
. Assuming
|\mathcal{S}|
is small enough, we may approximate
\sin(\chi)\approx 0
and
\cos(\chi)\approx 1
.

Inspired by the choice of modes in Eqs. (
38
) and (
39
) for the optimal state in with a finite number of modes presented in Sec.
E.3
, we set

\displaystyle\delta_{+,p}

\displaystyle=\Delta\cdot\sqrt{\frac{s_{2}^{2}}{s_{1}^{2}}}

(471)

\displaystyle\delta_{-,p}

\displaystyle=\Delta\cdot\sqrt{\frac{s_{1}^{2}}{s_{2}^{2}}},

(472)

where
\Delta>0
.

Now, we find

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{11}

\displaystyle\approx G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}=p_{0}+\delta_{+,p}

(473)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{22}

\displaystyle\approx G_{00}^{\Phi_{-}}=p_{0}-\delta_{-,p}

(474)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{12}

\displaystyle\approx 0+O(|\mathcal{S}|)

(475)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{23}

\displaystyle\approx 0+O(|\mathcal{S}|)

(476)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{14}

\displaystyle\approx 0+O(|\mathcal{S}|)

(477)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{31}

\displaystyle\approx G_{10}^{\Phi_{+}}=+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}=+\frac{i}{2\sigma_{z}}

(478)

\displaystyle\tilde{G}_{42}

\displaystyle\approx G_{10}^{\Phi_{-}}=+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\sigma}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}=+\frac{i}{2\sigma_{z}}.

(479)

Completely analogous to the finite case in Sec.
E.3
, we find

\displaystyle\bar{g}=+p_{0}

(480)

and

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\left[\left((p_{0}+\delta_{+,p})^{2}+0+\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}+0\right)+\left(0+(p_{0}-\delta_{-,p})^{2}+0+\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}\right)\right]\frac{N_{S}}{2}

(481)

\displaystyle=p_{0}^{2}+\Delta^{2}+\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}

(482)

from which follows

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}=\Delta^{2}+\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}.

(483)

In contrast to the finite dimensional case in Sec.
E.3
, here the extra term
\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}
appears due to regularization, which we assume to be negligible.

Until now, we have not specified the squeezing parameter. Inspired by Eqs. (
35
) and (
36
), we choose
r_{1},r_{2}
such that

\displaystyle s_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1-\frac{\bar{g}}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta^{2}}}\right)

(484)

\displaystyle s_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left(1+\frac{\bar{g}}{\sqrt{\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta^{2}}}\right).

(485)

With this, the calculation for the QFI is equivalent to the one in Sec.
E.3

and we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\left(8p_{0}^{2}+4\Delta^{2}\right)N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})

(486)

\displaystyle=\left(8p_{0}^{2}+4\left[\Delta g^{2}-\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}\right]\right)N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S}),

(487)

where again compared to the finite dimensional case in Sec.
E.3
the extra term
\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}
appears due to regularization.

I.0.2
QFI of variance optimal regularized state

Let us consider the regularized variance-optimal state. A variance-optimal state employs equal squeezing in both modes, i.e.,
r_{+}=r_{-}
from which follows
r_{1}=r_{2}
. We also have
\delta_{+,p}=\delta_{-,p}=:\delta
. In this regime, we have
\chi\approx\pi/4
(assuming
|\mathcal{S}|
sufficiently small) and thus
\cos(\chi)\approx\sin(\chi)\approx 1/\sqrt{2}
. The photon number in both populated Schmidt modes is then

\displaystyle s_{1}^{2}=s_{2}^{2}=N_{S}/2.

(488)

Now, we find that
\tilde{G}_{11}=\tilde{G}_{22}=(G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}+G_{00}^{\Phi_{-}})(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}})^{2}=(p_{0}+\delta+p_{0}-\delta)\frac{1}{2}=p_{0}
. From this follows

\displaystyle\bar{g}\approx+p_{0}.

(489)

Note that we have
\tilde{G}_{12}=\tilde{G}_{21}\approx(G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}}-G_{00}^{\Phi_{+}})(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}})^{2}=(p_{0}+\delta-(p_{0}-\delta))\frac{1}{2}=\delta
. We also have
|\tilde{G}_{13}|^{2}\approx|\tilde{G}_{14}|^{2}\approx|\tilde{G}_{24}|^{2}\approx|\tilde{G}_{23}|^{2}\approx|G_{10}^{\Phi_{\pm}}|^{2}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}})^{2}=\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}}(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}})^{2}(\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}})^{2}=\frac{1}{2^{3}\sigma^{2}}

We then find

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}+\bar{g}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\left[\left(|\tilde{G}_{11}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{12}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{13}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{14}|^{2}\right)s_{1}^{2}+\left(|\tilde{G}_{21}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{22}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{23}|^{2}+|\tilde{G}_{24}|^{2}\right)s_{2}^{2}\right]

(490)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\left[\left(p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{2^{3}\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2^{3}\sigma^{2}}\right)s_{1}^{2}+\left(p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{2^{3}\sigma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2^{3}\sigma^{2}}\right)s_{2}^{2}\right]

(491)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\frac{N_{S}}{2}\left[\left(p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{2^{2}\sigma^{2}}\right)+\left(p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{2^{2}\sigma^{2}}\right)\right]

(492)

\displaystyle=p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{2^{2}\sigma^{2}}

(493)

so that

\displaystyle\Delta g^{2}=\delta^{2}+\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}

(494)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\delta^{2}=\Delta g^{2}-\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}.

(495)

Now, the QFI is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)

\displaystyle\approx 8\left[|G_{11}|^{2}s_{1}^{4}+|G_{22}|^{2}s_{2}^{4}+s_{1}^{2}s_{2}^{2}(|G_{12}|^{2}+\text{Re}[G_{12}^{2}])\right]+O(N_{S})

(496)

\displaystyle=8\frac{N_{S}^{2}}{4}\left[p_{0}^{2}+p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}+\delta^{2}\right]+O(N_{S})=4N_{S}^{2}\left[p_{0}^{2}+\delta^{2}\right]+O(N_{S})

(497)

\displaystyle=4N_{S}^{2}\left[\bar{g}^{2}+\Delta g^{2}-\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}\right]+O(N_{S})

(498)

In the limit of
\Delta g^{2}\gg\frac{1}{4\sigma_{z}^{2}}
, the state is variance optimal.

Above we have assumed that
r_{1}=r_{2}
from which follows
\chi\approx\pi/4
. Due to experimental imperfections, the equality
r_{1}-r_{2}
may be slightly perturbed so that
|r_{1}-r_{2}|=\epsilon
. If
|\mathcal{S}|
is small enough so that
\epsilon\gg 2r_{-}|\mathcal{S}|^{2}
we have
\chi\ll 1
so that
\sin(\chi)\approx 0
and
\cos(\chi)\approx 1
. In that case, the state is still variance optimal, as can be shown with a similar calculation to the one presented in the previous Section
I.0.1
.

Appendix J
Optimal homodyne measurement for regularized state

Let us now propose an optimal measurement for the regularized optimal two-mode squeezed state discussed in Appendix
I
for the mode transform
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=e^{-a_{z}i\hat{G}_{a_{z}}}
. The measurement scheme is analogous to the optimal homodyne scheme for the discrete case presented in
F.1
.

The squeezing matrix is
f(z,z^{\prime})=r_{-}\Phi_{-,0}(z)\Phi_{-,0}(z^{\prime})+r_{+}\Phi_{+,0}(z)\Phi_{+,0}(z^{\prime})
with
\Phi_{\pm,n}(z)
as defined in Eq. (
428
).
Recall that the two modes
\Phi_{+,0}(z)
and
\Phi_{-,0}(z)
are orthogonal to a very good approximation under our assumptions given in
I
.

We denote
\hat{c}_{\pm,n}^{\dagger}(a_{z})=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{\pm,n}(z+a_{z})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)
and note that the transformed state is given by
|\psi_{a_{z}}\rangle\approx\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{+,0}(a_{z})}(r_{+})\hat{S}_{\hat{c}_{-,0}(a_{z})}(r_{-})|0\rangle
, where the approximate sign is due to the fact that the modes are only approximately orthogonal.

Now, let us assume we have a prior guess
a_{z,\text{prior}}
of
a_{z}
.
We propose to homodyne the modes
\hat{c}_{+,0}(a_{z,\text{prior}})
and
\hat{c}_{+,0}(a_{z,\text{prior}})
. We assume that our prior guess
a_{z,\text{prior}}=a_{z}+\epsilon
is good enough so that

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z,\text{prior}})

\displaystyle\approx\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})+\epsilon\partial_{y}\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(y)\Big|_{y=a_{z}}

(499)

\displaystyle=\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})+\epsilon\,(-i)^{*}\left[G^{\Phi_{\pm}*}_{00}\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})+G^{\Phi_{\pm}*}_{10}\hat{c}_{\pm,1}(a_{z})\right]

(500)

\displaystyle=\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})+\epsilon\,(-i)^{*}\left[(p_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,p})\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})+\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}}\hat{c}_{\pm,1}(a_{z})\right]

(501)

\displaystyle\approx\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})e^{+i\epsilon(p_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,p})}+\epsilon\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}}\hat{c}_{\pm,1}(a_{z}),

(502)

where we used Eq. (
452
) and Eq. (
400
).

Now, we propose to measure the two quadratures

\displaystyle\hat{x}_{\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z,\text{prior}})e^{-i\varphi_{\pm}}}:=\frac{\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z,\text{prior}})e^{-i\varphi_{\pm}}+\hat{c}_{\pm,0}^{\dagger}(a_{z,\text{prior}})e^{+i\varphi_{\pm}}}{\sqrt{2}}

(503)

Now, from this point on, we neglect all terms of order
\epsilon^{2}
, which is legitimate assuming we have a good enough prior. Then, the analysis is completely analogous to the analysis of the finite mode setting discussed in the Appendix
F.1
. This is due to the fact that the expectation of terms like
\hat{c}_{\pm,1}
,
\hat{c}_{\pm,1}\hat{c}_{\pm,0}
,
\hat{c}_{\pm,1}\hat{c}_{\mp,0}
and
\hat{c}_{\pm,1}\hat{c}_{\mp,1}
is zero, as our squeezed state has no population in
\hat{c}_{\pm,1}
. The expectation of terms quadratic in
\hat{c}_{\pm,1}
is non zero, however, these terms are always accompanied by a prefactor
\epsilon^{2}
, which is why we can neglect them.
Thus, the only relevant operators in the analysis become
\hat{c}_{\pm,0}(a_{z})e^{+i\epsilon(p_{0}\pm\delta_{\pm,p})}
like in Appendix
F.1
.

Repeating all the steps from Appendix
F.1
then leads us to

\displaystyle\max_{\varphi_{+},\varphi_{-}}F_{\text{hom}}(|\psi_{a_{z}}\rangle)=8(p_{0}+\delta_{+,p})^{2}s_{+}^{2}(s_{+}^{2}+1)+8(p_{0}-\delta_{-,p})^{2}s_{-}^{2}(s_{-}^{2}+1)

(504)

with
s_{\pm}=\sinh(r_\pm)
.

Now by using the relations between the parameters
p_{0}
,
\delta_{\pm,p}
and the resource parameters
\bar{p}
,
\Delta p
given in
I.0.1
for the optimal state and given in
I.0.2
, we can show that the above FI equals its respective QFI. Thus, the homodyne measurement scheme presented here is optimal for the regularized optimal state from
I.0.1
and the variance-optimal state from
I.0.2
.

We can also introduce loss by modeling it as a beam splitter of transmissivity
\eta_{\pm}
, and the analysis is completely analogous to the one presented in Appendix
F.2
.

Appendix K
Variance-optimal measurement for shift mode transforms

Recall from Definition
III.3
that
variance-optimal measurements are measurements whose FI fully attains the variance contribution of the QFI. So, say we have a state
|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle
whose QFI is
\mathcal{F}(|\psi_{\lambda}\rangle)=c_{\bar{g}}\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+c_{\Delta g}\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
. A variance-optimal measurement then attains an FI of
c_{\bar{g}}^{\prime}\bar{g}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+c_{\Delta g}^{\prime}\Delta g^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
with
c_{\Delta g}^{\prime}=c_{\Delta g}
and
c_{\bar{g}}^{\prime}\leq c_{\bar{g}}
.

Let us consider the mode parameter shift transformation in the
z
domain. The analysis of a shift in the
p
domain is analogous due to Fourier duality.
So we consider

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{a_{z}}=e^{-ia_{z}\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p)}

(505)

which has the effect

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{F}(p)\hat{U}_{a_{z}}

\displaystyle=\hat{F}(p)e^{-ia_{z}p}

(506)

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{a_{z}}

\displaystyle=\hat{D}(z+a_{z}).

(507)

Also recall the unitary of a shift in the
p
domain with unitary
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}=e^{-ia_{p}\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z)}
that acts as
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{a_{p}}=\hat{D}(z)e^{-ia_{p}z}
and
\hat{U}_{a_{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{F}(p)\hat{U}_{a_{p}}=\hat{F}(p-a_{p})
.

Now, any state
|\psi\rangle
is characterized by mean parameters
\bar{z},\bar{p}
, one in
z
and the other in its Fourier dual
p
space. We have

\displaystyle\bar{z}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\frac{\langle\psi|\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z)|\psi\rangle}{N_{S}}

(508)

\displaystyle\bar{p}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\frac{\langle\psi|\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p)|\psi\rangle}{N_{S}}

(509)

So, let us denote

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle=:|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle=\hat{U}_{-\bar{z}}\hat{U}_{+\bar{p}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle,

(510)

explicitly indicating the mean resource parameters of the respective state.
Here,
|\psi(0,0)\rangle
is a version of the state
|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle
whose mean resource parameters are both zero. Note that

\displaystyle|\psi(0,0)\rangle=\hat{U}_{-\bar{p}}\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(511)

Let us show this:

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\langle\psi(0,0)|\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z)|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(512)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{-\bar{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{-\bar{p}}\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(513)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle e^{-i\bar{p}z}e^{+i\bar{p}z}

(514)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z)\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(515)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,z\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z+\bar{z})\hat{D}(z+\bar{z})|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(516)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}z\,(z-\bar{z})\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}(z)|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(517)

\displaystyle=(\bar{z}-\bar{z})N_{S}

(518)

\displaystyle=0

(519)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\langle\psi(0,0)|\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p)|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(520)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{-\bar{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p)\hat{U}_{-\bar{p}}\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(521)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p-(-\bar{p}))\hat{F}(p-(-\bar{p}))\hat{U}_{+\bar{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(522)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}p\,p\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p+\bar{p})\hat{F}(p+\bar{p})|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle e^{-i\bar{z}(p+\bar{p})}e^{+i\bar{z}(p+\bar{p})}

(523)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}p\,(p-\bar{p})\langle\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})|\hat{F}^{\dagger}(p)\hat{F}(p)|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(524)

\displaystyle=(\bar{p}-\bar{p})N_{S}

(525)

\displaystyle=0.

(526)

K.1
The variance-optimal measurement condition

In the following we want to show that resolved photon counting in the
z
domain can be optimal if the state satisfies the variance-optimal measurement condition we are about to introduce. Resolved photon counting in the
z
domain corresponds to projecting the quantum state onto

\displaystyle|\bm{z}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\otimes_{i=1}^{K}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z_{i})|0\rangle

(527)

where
\bm{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{K})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and
K\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

The variance-optimal measurement condition is as follows:

Lemma K.1
.

If a quantum state of interest
|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial_{a_{z}}\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]=0

(528)

for all
\bm{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
and all
K\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\displaystyle F(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle,\{|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\})=\mathcal{F}(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)).

(529)

Let us explain the above lemma. The condition is essentially that the measurement amplitudes’ phase is independent of the parameter
a_{z}
to be estimated. If a state satisfies this condition, then the corresponding FI of this state
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle
(centered at
\bar{p}
in the
p
domain) for the measurement
\{|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\}
equals the QFI of the state
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle
centered at
0
in the
p
domain. To clarify what this means, suppose that the state under consideration has a QFI of
\mathcal{F}(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0))=c_{\bar{p}}\bar{p}^{2}N_{S}^{2}+c_{\Delta p}\Delta p^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
(see Eq. (
33
)). If this state satisfies our condition, then the FI of this state for the measurement
\{|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\}
is
F(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle,\{|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\})=c_{\Delta p}\Delta p^{2}N_{S}^{2}+O(N_{S})
. That is, the FI attains the variance term of the QFI, but does not attain the mean term. Such a measurement is called variance optimal according to our Definition
III.3
.

Let us now proof lemma
K.1
.

Proof.

Let us consider

\displaystyle\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle=\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{-\bar{z}}\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle=\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}-\bar{z}}\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(530)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\langle 0|[\otimes_{i=1}^{K}\hat{D}(z_{i})]\hat{U}_{a_{z}-\bar{z}}\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\underbrace{\langle 0|\hat{U}_{a_{z}-\bar{z}}}_{=\langle 0|}[\otimes_{i=1}^{K}\hat{U}_{a_{z}-\bar{z}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z_{i})\hat{U}_{a_{z}-\bar{z}}]\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(531)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\langle 0|[\otimes_{i=1}^{K}\hat{D}(z_{i}+a_{z}-\bar{z})]\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\underbrace{\langle 0|\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}}_{=\langle 0|}[\otimes_{i=1}^{K}\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}^{\dagger}\hat{D}(z_{i}+a_{z}-\bar{z})\hat{U}_{\bar{p}}]|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(532)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\langle 0|\hat{[}\otimes_{i=1}^{K}e^{-i\bar{p}(z_{i}+a_{z}-\bar{z})}\hat{D}(z_{i}+a_{z}-\bar{z})]|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(533)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{K}e^{-i\bar{p}(z_{i}+a_{z}-\bar{z})}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{K!}}\langle 0|[\otimes_{i=1}^{K}\hat{D}(z_{i})]\hat{U}_{a_{z}-\bar{z}}|\psi(0,0)\rangle

(534)

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{K}e^{-i\bar{p}(z_{i}+a_{z}-\bar{z})}\right)\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle

(535)

From the above, we see that
\bar{p}
only enter as a phase into the amplitudes, and thus the measurement probabilities will be independent of
\bar{p}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle|^{2}=|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle|^{2}=:p(\bm{z}|a_{z}).

(536)

Let us denote

\displaystyle\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=:qe^{i\xi},

(537)

where we defined
q=|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle|
as the magnitude and
\xi=\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]
as the phase. The argument function is defined as
\arg[z]=\arg[|z|e^{i\phi}]=\phi
.

Next, we want to calculate the FI of the state
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle
using resolved photon counting in the
z
domain. First note that we have

\displaystyle F(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle,\{|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\})

\displaystyle=F(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle,\{|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\})

(538)

\displaystyle=\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\frac{(\partial_{a_{z}}p(\bm{z}|a_{z}))^{2}}{p(\bm{z}|a_{z})}=\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\frac{(\partial_{a_{z}}q^{2})^{2}}{q^{2}}=\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\frac{(2q\partial_{a_{z}}q)^{2}}{q^{2}}=4\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z(\partial_{a_{z}}q)^{2}

(539)

\displaystyle=4\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\left(\partial_{a_{z}}\big|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle\big|\right)^{2}

(540)

\displaystyle=4\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\big|\langle\bm{z}|\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle\big|^{2}=4\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|(\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}})^{\dagger}\left[\int\kern-13.50008pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\right]\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle

(541)

\displaystyle=4\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|(\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}})^{\dagger}\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle

(542)

\displaystyle=4\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|(\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}})^{\dagger}\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle-4\underbrace{|\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle|^{2}}_{=0}

(543)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0))

(544)

where the symbol
\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z
means that we sum the integrals over all the photon number subspaces. In the fourth line we used
\left(\partial_{a_{z}}\big|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle\big|\right)^{2}=\big|\langle\bm{z}|\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle\big|^{2}
. This follows from
\langle\bm{z}|\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\partial_{a_{z}}\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\partial_{a_{z}}(qe^{i\xi})=e^{i\xi}\partial_{a_{z}}q=e^{i\xi}\partial_{a_{z}}|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle|
. Taking the squared modulus
|\cdot|^{2}
of this chain of equalities and noting that
|e^{i\xi}\partial_{a_{z}}|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle||^{2}=|\partial_{a_{z}}|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle||^{2}=(\partial_{a_{z}}|\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle|)^{2}
proves the used relation.
We also used in the fourth line we used
\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|=\hat{\mathbb{1}}
. In the sixth line we used
|\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle|^{2}=0
which follows from
0=\partial_{a_{z}}\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\,p(\bm{z}|a_{z})=\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\,\partial_{a_{z}}q^{2}=\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\,2q\partial_{a_{z}}q=\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\,2qe^{-i\xi}\partial_{a_{z}}(qe^{i\xi})=2\int\kern-10.00002pt\sum\mathrm{d}^{k}z\,\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}|\bm{z}\rangle\langle\bm{z}|\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\langle\psi(\bar{z},0)|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}^{\dagger}\partial_{a_{z}}\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle
, where we used the compleness relation and
\partial_{a_{z}}\xi=0
. With this, our proof is complete.
∎

K.2
Determining if a state satisfies the variance-optimal measurement condition

So, let us now check when the condition
\partial_{a_{z}}\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]=0
is satisfied for some arbitrary multi-mode squeezed vacuum state. Let us write the state in the Schmidt mode basis
\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Psi_{n}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)
in which the state appears in product form:

\displaystyle|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n}\exp\left(+\frac{r_{n}e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}-\frac{r_{n}e^{-i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{2}\right)|0\rangle

(545)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n}\exp\left(+\frac{\tanh(r_n)e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}\right)|0\rangle

(546)

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n}\exp\left(+\frac{\tanh(r_n)e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Psi_{n}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,\Psi_{n}(\tilde{z})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(\tilde{z})\right)|0\rangle

(547)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int\mathrm{d}z\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\left[\sum_{n}\frac{\tanh(r_n)e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\Psi_{n}(z)\Psi_{n}(\tilde{z})\right]\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(\tilde{z})\right)|0\rangle

(548)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int\mathrm{d}z\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}\,g(z,\tilde{z})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(\tilde{z})\right)|0\rangle

(549)

Now, let us gain some insight and first look at a simple two-photon example:

\displaystyle\langle(z,\tilde{z})^{T}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\langle 0|\hat{D}(z)\hat{D}(\tilde{z})|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(550)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\langle 0|\hat{D}(z)\hat{D}(\tilde{z})\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\prime}\,g(z^{\prime},\tilde{z}^{\prime})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})|0\rangle

(551)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\prime}\,g(z^{\prime},\tilde{z}^{\prime})\langle 0|\hat{D}(z)\hat{D}(\tilde{z})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z^{\prime})|0\rangle

(552)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\prime}\,g(z^{\prime},\tilde{z}^{\prime})\left[\delta(z-z^{\prime})\delta(\tilde{z}-\tilde{z}^{\prime})+\delta(z-\tilde{z}^{\prime})\delta(\tilde{z}-z^{\prime})\right]

(553)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\left[g(z,\tilde{z})+g(\tilde{z},z)\right].

(554)

For the parameter imprinted state we have

\displaystyle\langle(z,\tilde{z})^{T}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2!}}\left[g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})+g(\tilde{z}+a_{z},z+a_{z})\right].

(555)

Now, for an arbitrary
z
-resolving photon counting measurement, we find

\displaystyle\langle\bm{z}|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2K)!}}\langle 0|[\otimes_{i=1}^{2K}\hat{D}(z_{i})]|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

(556)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2K)!}}\frac{1}{K!}\int\mathrm{d}z_{1}^{\prime}\ldots\int\mathrm{d}z_{2K}^{\prime}\,\,g(z_{1}^{\prime},z_{2}^{\prime})\cdots g(z_{2K-1}^{\prime},z_{2K}^{\prime})\langle 0|[\otimes_{i=1}^{2K}\hat{D}(z_{i})][\otimes_{i=1}^{2K}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z_{i}^{\prime})]|0\rangle

(557)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2K)!}}\frac{1}{K!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{2K}}\prod_{b=1}^{K}g(z_{\pi(2b-1)},z_{\pi(2b)}).

(558)

Here,
\sum_{\pi\in S_{2K}}
means to sum over all permutations
\pi
of the indices
\{1,\ldots,2K\}
.

Let us now consider various states and see if they satisfy the condition for a variance-optimal measurement.

K.2.1
Phase-insensitive resolved photon counting measurement for product of single-mode squeezed vacuua in the HG mode basis

Let us consider

\displaystyle|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n}\exp\left(+\frac{r_{n}e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}-\frac{r_{n}e^{-i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{2}\right)|0\rangle

(559)

where

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)

(560)

is the HG mode basis with

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(z)=\Phi(z;z_{0},p_{0},\sigma,\theta)

(561)

It is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle\bar{z}

\displaystyle=z_{0}

(562)

\displaystyle\bar{p}

\displaystyle=p_{0}

(563)

regardless of how the squeezing
r_{n}
is distributed among the modes.

Now, it follows that the state
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\bigotimes_{n}\exp\left(+\frac{r_{n}e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{\prime{\dagger}2}-\frac{r_{n}e^{-i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{\prime 2}\right)|0\rangle
has the same form, but with the mode operators

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{n}^{\prime{\dagger}}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Phi_{n}(z+a_{z};z_{0},0,\sigma,\theta_{n})\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)

(564)

Now, let us consider:

\displaystyle\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2K)!}}\frac{1}{K!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{2K}}\prod_{b=1}^{K}g(z_{\pi(2b-1)}+a_{z},z_{\pi(2b)}+a_{z}).

(565)

We then find

\displaystyle g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})=\sum_{n}\frac{\tanh(r_n)}{2}\Phi_{n}(z+a_{z};z_{0},0,\sigma,\theta_{n})(z)\Phi_{n}(\bar{z}+a_{z};z_{0},0,\sigma,\theta_{n})

(566)

Now, for
\theta=0
,
g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})
is real, and thus,
\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle
is real valued as well. So the condition
\partial_{a_{z}}\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]=0
is satisfied.

Similarly,
\theta_{n}=\theta
for all
n
, we have
\arg g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})=-2\theta
from which follows
\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]=-2K\theta
, and the condition is also satisfied. In addition, one can show that the condition can be satisfied if
\theta_{n}
differ from one another by integer multiples of
\pi
.

For the general case
\theta_{n}
, the condition is no longer generally satisfied.

K.2.2
Phase-insensitive measurement for the optimal state

Let us now consider our optimal state and examine under what conditions phase-insensitive measurements can be variance optimal.
Again, the state can be written as

\displaystyle|\psi(\bar{z},\bar{p})\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigotimes_{n=\{i,j\}}\exp\left(+\frac{r_{n}e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{{\dagger}2}-\frac{r_{n}e^{-i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{2}\right)|0\rangle

(567)

with

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{n}^{\dagger}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Psi_{n}(z)\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z).

(568)

Recall that the optimal states from Sec.
I
has Schmidt modes fo the form

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0}(z;z_{1},p_{1},\sigma_{z},\theta_{1})+O(|\mathcal{S}|)

(569)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0}(z;z_{2},p_{2},\sigma_{z},\theta_{2})+O(|\mathcal{S}|)

(570)

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(z)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}(z;z_{1},p_{1},\sigma_{z},\theta_{1})+O(|\mathcal{S}|)

(571)

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}(z)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}(z;z_{2},p_{2},\sigma_{z},\theta_{2})+O(|\mathcal{S}|).

(572)

Now assume that like in Sec.
I
that

\displaystyle p_{1}

\displaystyle=p_{0}+\delta_{+,p}

(573)

\displaystyle p_{2}

\displaystyle=p_{0}-\delta_{-,p},

(574)

and our choice of
r_{n}
shall ensure that

\displaystyle\bar{p}=p_{0}.

(575)

How to achieve this was discussed in Sections
I.0.1
and
I.0.2
.

Now again, the state
\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\bigotimes_{n}\exp\left(+\frac{r_{n}e^{i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{\prime{\dagger}2}-\frac{r_{n}e^{-i\phi_{n}}}{2}\hat{c}_{n}^{\prime 2}\right)|0\rangle
has the same form, but with the mode operators

\displaystyle\hat{c}_{n}^{\prime{\dagger}}=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Psi_{n}(z+a_{z})e^{+i\bar{p}(z+a_{z})}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)=\int\mathrm{d}z\,\Psi_{n}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}\hat{D}^{\dagger}(z)

(576)

and we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0}(z;z_{1},+\delta_{+,p},\sigma_{z},\theta_{1})

(577)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0}(z;z_{2},-\delta_{-,p},\sigma_{z},\theta_{2})

(578)

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}(z;z_{1},+\delta_{+,p},\sigma_{z},\theta_{1})

(579)

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}(z;z_{2},-\delta_{-,p},\sigma_{z},\theta_{2}).

(580)

Now, let us consider:

\displaystyle\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2K)!}}\frac{1}{K!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{2K}}\prod_{b=1}^{K}g(z_{\pi(2b-1)}+a_{z},z_{\pi(2b)}+a_{z})

(581)

Now, let us assume
z_{1}=z_{2}=z_{0}
(as we will see, this choice ensures that certain terms can be factored out):

\displaystyle g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})

\displaystyle=+\frac{\tanh(r_1)}{2}\Psi_{1}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}\Psi_{1}(\tilde{z}+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(\tilde{z}+a_{z})}

(582)

\displaystyle\quad\,\,+\frac{\tanh(r_2)}{2}\Psi_{2}(z+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(z+a_{z})}\Psi_{2}(\tilde{z}+a_{z})e^{+ip_{0}(\tilde{z}+a_{z})}

(583)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}\pi}\right)^{2/4}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}}^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{z+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\tilde{z}+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}

(584)

\displaystyle\times\Big[t_{1}e^{-i(p_{0}+\delta_{+,p})(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z}-2z_{0})}e^{-i2\theta_{1}}\cdot e^{+ip_{0}(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z})}+t_{2}e^{-i(p_{0}-\delta_{-,p})(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z}-2z_{0})}e^{-i2\theta_{2}}\cdot e^{+ip_{0}(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z})}\Big]

(585)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}\pi}\right)^{2/4}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}}^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{z+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\tilde{z}+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}

(586)

\displaystyle\times e^{+2ip_{0}z_{0}}\Big[t_{1}e^{-i\delta_{+,p}(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z}-2z_{0})}e^{-i2\theta_{1}}+t_{2}e^{+i\delta_{-,p}(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z}-2z_{0})}e^{-i2\theta_{2}}\Big]

(587)

where
t_{n}=\tanh(r_n)
.

Now, if we pick
\delta_{+,p}=\delta_{-,p}=\delta
,
r_{1}=r_{2}
, and also
\theta_{1}=\theta_{2}=\theta
, it follows that

\displaystyle g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}\pi}\right)^{2/4}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}}^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{z+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\tilde{z}+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}\tanh(r)

(588)

\displaystyle\times e^{+i2p_{0}z_{0}}e^{-i2\theta}\Big[e^{+i\delta(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z}-2z_{0})}+e^{-i\delta(z+\tilde{z}+2a_{z}-2z_{0})}\Big]

(589)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma_{z}^{2}\pi}\right)^{2/4}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-n}}{n!}}^{2}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{z+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\tilde{z}+a_{z}-z_{0}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{z}}\right)^{2}}\tanh(r)

(590)

\displaystyle\times e^{+i2p_{0}z_{0}}e^{-i2\theta}\cdot 2\cos( \delta(z+\tilde z+ 2a_z- 2z_0)).

(591)

We thus find that
\arg[g(z+a_{z},\tilde{z}+a_{z})]=2(p_{0}z_{0}-\theta)
, and therefore
\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\Psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]=2K(p_{0}z_{0}-\theta)
, from which it follows that
\partial_{a_{z}}\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]=0
. Note that the choice
\theta_{1}=\theta
and
\theta_{2}=\theta+\pi
leads to an analogous result, with a
\sin
dependence replacing the
\cos
.

The conditions for a variance-optimal measurement thus require equal squeezing
r_{1}=r_{2}
, together with
\delta_{-}=\delta_{+}
and
z_{1}=z_{2}
. For
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
, one generally has
\partial_{a_{z}}\arg[\langle\bm{z}|\hat{U}_{a_{z}}|\psi(\bar{z},0)\rangle]\neq 0
, and the variance-optimality condition is no longer satisfied.
